Apple HomePod mini 2 review: A necessary, but underwhelming update

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I'd be willing to forgive you if you forgot about the new HomePod mini. The overhauled smart speaker played a supporting role at best during Apple's Welcome Home event last month, behind the HomeView, and probably, in some minds, behind the updated Apple TV 4K. Still, the company provides a few upgrades in the HomePod mini 2 ($149), ensuring that it's ready for Siri AI and the assistant's newfound ability to offer personalized context for your queries. The main problem? The speaker doesn't yet support the recently upgraded virtual assistant Apple spent years improving.

The most notable update on the HomePod mini 2 is on the inside. Apple swapped in its S10 chip from the Apple Watch Series 10, kicking the nearly antique S5 silicon to the curb. For a Watch comparison, that's like going from a Series 5 or first-gen Watch SE, which debuted in 2019 and 2020 respectively, to the 2024 edition. It's a big leap, and it's one Apple says was necessary for the HomePod mini to work with Siri AI.

The recently updated assistant isn't all the S10 is helping with here. Apple says the component can run real-time music evaluation "to optimize dynamic range, loudness and acoustic performance," with a hand from "advanced computational analysis." When connected to the newly introduced fourth-gen Apple TV 4K, you can use four HomePod mini 2 speakers as a surround sound setup. That S10 chip helps the speakers recognize their positions in a room and adjust the lag, convey that info to Apple's streaming box, and audio levels accordingly.

I only have two HomePod mini 2s, so I wasn't able to test this configuration, but I did hear it during Apple's event in October. Even with that brief experience, I can tell you using two second-gen HomePods as the front speakers and two HomePod minis as the rears is a much better setup. The HomePod mini doesn't muster enough bass to be a great home theater speaker on its own, and those full-size HomePods bring the boom when an action scene demands it.

Before now, you could send audio from a Mac to a HomePod mini, but Apple has expanded the functionality so that the second-gen model can serve as persistent default speakers. This means, in theory, they'll stay connected to your Mac as the primary output rather than just relying on a temporary AirPlay link. This allows you to use the HomePod mini 2, or a pair of them, for everything on your computer — from FaceTime calls to games — without having to think about connecting each app or session to the speaker(s).

The HomePod line has always had lights on the top, but they haven't really done much. Before now, the top touch panel would light up if audio was playing or display an animation when you chatted with Siri. With a new feature called Sound Glow, those lights now show hues that compliment a song's album artwork. The lights move in real time, reacting to the bass and treble in each tune. If you opt for a soundscape, Sound Glow reacts to that too, showing an animation that resembles the pitter patter of rain drops, for example. It isn't a game-changing feature, but it's a nice use of lighting that was barely utilized previously.

Lastly, there are the new colors. The HomePod mini 2 comes in pink, green, blue, white and midnight (dark grey), with those first three coordinating well with Apple's iMac colors. That list means the company retired orange and yellow from the previous generation, both of which looked great in their time. As you can see, my review units are green, and I've enjoyed the pop of color they've added to my desk over the last few weeks.

Given that Apple saw fit to bestow a "2" on this HomePod mini, I expected some upgrades to overall sound quality to be in the mix. Unfortunately, I can't notice a difference between old and new models. Sometimes I think there's some variation, but it's not substantial enough that I'm sure, and it's not consistent across various musical styles. I do like the audio performance of the HomePod mini for its size, but it still has a sound profile of crisp highs and prominent mids offset by bass tone that lacks dynamics and depth. It's good enough for casual listening or background tunes, but not for anything more than that.

Despite the lack of Siri AI, I was hoping the S10 chip would provide a performance boost for the old Siri experience you have to settle for right now. However, the assistant's responses to basic questions took the same amount of time as they do on the first-gen model. To be clear, they're quick on both devices, I'm merely pointing out there's no difference in this regard. Just don't try to ask the old assistant to dive into Apple's core apps for you, or try to have an on-going conversation — neither request will bear any fruit.

The biggest issue with the HomePod mini 2 right now is its biggest omission: Siri AI. The recently updated assistant isn't available on the speaker yet, and Apple hasn't provided a timeline for its arrival either. The upgraded HomePod mini is equipped to support the now-current-gen Siri, thanks to the S10 chip. However, you'll have to wait for Siri's newfound contextual awareness to dive into your Messages, Calendar and other apps on your iPhone or iPad. And to me, that's a huge miss for Apple on an overhauled smart speaker. You'll also have to wait for the more conversational chats with Siri as the HomePod mini 2 doesn't yet support those either.

I like the idea of using a HomePod mini 2 or a stereo pair of them as a persistent computer speaker with a Mac, but it's not a great experience in practice. Any time you do anything — start a song, pause a song, skip tracks — there's a 1-2 second delay in the audio responding. The lights on the top of the speakers, including the Sound Glow effect, will stop shining immediately, but the audio has to catch up. There's also a slight delay between speakers if you're using a stereo pair, which sounds like every song has added reverb. For these reasons, I found the Mac speaker feature more frustrating than enjoyable, and I quickly moved back to AirPlay over a nearby Sonos speaker for my productivity tunes. And I haven't even mentioned the fact that I can't get the persistent connection to stick with my MacBook Air either. I'm constantly reselecting the HomePod mini 2 as the audio output.

Another item that's disappointing is something else that's not here: a substantial overhaul. The original HomePod mini is six years old at this point, so I expected a much bigger redesign for the second-gen version (until the rumor mill squashed that). If you have a first-gen HomePod mini, you'll miss out on the updated Siri, whenever it arrives, and new features like Sound Glow and the home theater option. And if you're hoping to create a stereo pair, you'll need two of the same generation HomePod minis to do that; you can't pair a first-gen speaker with a second-gen model for that purpose.

Lastly, there's the price. The previous version was $99 before Apple raised it to $129 in June when the company hiked the prices on pretty much all of its hardware. Now, I'm well aware of the global economic factors at play, especially the AI-fueled cost increase on RAM and other components, but asking $50 more than the first-gen model for incremental updates is tough. Had Apple kept the price at the June rate, the HomePod mini 2 would be a much easier recommendation.

It's baffling to me that Apple would announce an updated HomePod mini without support for the latest version of Siri ready to go. The speaker's primary use is a hands-free conduit to the company's voice assistant, after all. The only decent conclusion I can come to is that Apple wanted to announce what it considered was a full suite of home products around the HomeView debut, and it felt like the Apple TV 4K, HomePod mini and a collection of smart home devices were the best way to do that. I would be interested to know what the hold up is, because the Apple Watch Series 10 supports Siri AI already, and those wearables are also running the same S10 chip as the HomePod mini 2. But, of course, Apple isn't talking specifics.

After spending a few weeks with the HomePod mini 2, I mostly wish Apple had just waited. At a higher price, this isn't as compelling as it used to be in terms of getting Siri in your home. And with Siri AI missing, and some of the new audio features proving to be unreliable, it's clear a bit more polish was needed here.

Jamie and Corinne Foxx Developing Horror Series ‘Buried’ as Foxxhole Productions Extends First-Look Deal at Fox Entertainment Studios (EXCLUSIVE)

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Fox Entertainment Studios has extended its first-look agreement with Foxxhole Productions, the company led by Corinne Foxx and Jamie Foxx, to 2028.

Jamie Foxx signed the first-look deal last year; terms of the extension specify that he will serve as an executive producer and content originator for scripted programming spanning comedy, drama and animation, with Fox Entertainment Studios retaining first-look rights. The agreement also gives the studio exclusive rights to Foxx’s unscripted projects. Related Stories Queen Latifah, Bone Thugs-N-Harmony, MC Lyte and More to Lead 2027 Rock the Bells Cruise

The Academy Award-winning actor, comedian and producer’s relationship with the Fox network dates back to 1991, when he landed his breakout role on the sketch comedy series “In Living Color.” That success led to his eponymous sitcom, “The Jamie Foxx Show,” which ran for five seasons.

Under the pact, Foxxhole Productions and Fox will develop the horror series “Buried,” with “The Conjuring” creators Chad and Carey Hayes and Shelley Meals (“It: Welcome to Derry”).

“We’re thrilled to continue our partnership with Fox Entertainment Studios and to keep building together across both scripted and unscripted,” Foxxhole Productions president Corinne Foxx said in a statement. “Foxxhole Productions is focused on bold, character-driven projects that are commercial, entertaining and often center Black voices and experiences. ‘Buried’ is the perfect example of that. We’re excited to be working with Shelley Meals alongside masters of horror Chad and Carey Hayes, who together are the perfect team to make this story as emotionally resonant as it is haunting.”

“Buried” is inspired by the true story of the residents of a Crosby, Texas subdivision who reported experiencing hauntings before discovering their new homes were built atop Black Hope Cemetery, a long-forgotten burial ground for a Freedmen’s Town settlement. “The reported supernatural phenomena drew national attention, and some residents say paranormal events continue today,” a release announcing the series notes.

The Hayes Brothers will serve as executive producers on “Buried” alongside Jamie and Corinne Foxx, who will executive produce through Foxxhole Productions, with Christina Porter, VP of scripted development, overseeing development. Meals will serve as showrunner and executive producer on the project.

Peter Moore says this is "more than the release of a hugely anticipated game"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

As GTA 6 brings in rave reviews and – predictably – takes over the internet as millions of players dive into Rockstar's long-awaited blockbuster, industry veteran Peter Moore says "the video game industry could really use a moment like this."

Moore, who previously served as president of Sega of America, corporate vice president of Microsoft's home and entertainment division responsible for Xbox marketing and operations, and CEO of EA, has taken to Twitter in the wake of Rockstar's latest launch. First, he offers an AI-modified image of himself with a GTA 6 tattoo in place of his infamous GTA 4 tattoo, which was revealed at E3 2006 to confirm the game's simultaneous launch on Xbox 360 and PS3.

But "in all seriousness," he adds, GTA 6's launch is an important one, and not just for the fans who've been waiting 13 years to play a new Grand Theft Auto game.

"We've been through a challenging period," he acknowledges. "Studio closures, redundancies, canceled projects and enormous uncertainty have taken their toll on an industry that has given so much to so many, myself included.

"That's why the launch of GTA VI feels so significant," he continues. "It's more than the release of a hugely anticipated game. It's an opportunity to remind the world of the extraordinary power of this industry to create, innovate, entertain and bring people together."

As someone who's spent so much time in the games industry, Moore says, "I've seen this industry reinvent itself time and time again." With that in mind, he hopes GTA 6 is able to deliver "the commercial success we all expect, but perhaps more importantly, I hope it provides a much-needed shot in the arm (no pun intended…) for the entire ecosystem: developers, publishers, studios, retailers, technology partners and the next generation of creative talent." After all, he says, "The games industry has always been at its best when it dares to dream big."

Well, as far as things go on the commercial front, it looks like the game has had an extremely strong start. Rockstar hasn't revealed any official figures, but with analysts' old launch week predictions going as high as $5.2 billion, and more recent estimates placing the revenue as of yesterday at $1.65 billion, it's safe that plenty of folks are buying it.

MG U9 EV Ute — It’s A Monster Truck

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We visit the MG dealership and get to know the MG U9 EV ute up close and personal.

As I have said many times to CleanTechnica readers, Australia needs more BEV utes. The PHEV utes are taking a bite out of diesel sales, but we need some monster truck battery electrics to push electrification deep into the heart of the bush-bashing Aussie tradie. I think that MG may have achieved that with the launch of the MG U9 EV. Yesterday, Majella and I went to have a look. Even Russell Coight would be impressed and the Mitsubishi Outlander PHEV should be feeling threatened. Take a break and watch the funny video. I doubt it will affect the sales of the Shark though.

So, yesterday, Majella I set out to have an adventure visiting the various car yards near our home. We were actually planning to look at some new electric vans, but ended up in the MG showroom with a very knowledgeable sales rep exploring the MG U9 EV. What a surprise! An early morning google search assured me that there were none in Brisbane available to view. This dealership not only brought one out from the pre-delivery area for us to check out, but also told us they had already sold and delivered their first one and had six more on order.

The MG U9 EV (it also comes in diesel) is joining the thin ranks of BEV utes already available in Australia — the KGM Musso and the Toyota Hilux BEV. So far this year, 269 KGM Mussos have sold in Australia. 174 MG U9 EVs are listed as delivered, possibly mainly to dealers. The cost of the MG U9 EV is approximately AU$80,000, just a few thousand more than the Toyota Hilux BEV. The diesel version of the MG sells for around AU$50,000.

The sales rep told me about his first customer for the MG ute: “He was keen. He already had home solar and a battery and was producing far more electricity than he could use. His solution: trade in the old diesel ute on a U9 EV ‘rolling battery.’ It will complement his Kia EV6. With the 6.6 kWh of the U9 vehicle-to-load [capability], he plans to power his house.” The ecosystem expands. For those with a query, it is not worth selling power back to the grid.

The MG U9 has a 102.2 kWh LFP battery, big enough to give a range of up to 430 km and send this behemoth hurtling down the road — accelerating to 100 km an hour from zero in 5.8 seconds. That’s as fast as my new Tesla! And an advertised top speed of 190 km/hour. Just as well it has disc brakes all round — with the front ones ventilated. And to recharge — 42 minutes from 20–80%.

Tare weight comes in at 2815 kg, towing capacity is 3500 kg, and it has the capacity to carry 685 kg in the tray. Unladen ground clearance is listed as 230 mm, and it has a wading depth of 550 mm. Length is 5500 mm; width 2265 mm; height 1874 mm. Very similar dimensions to the GMC Hummer with half the battery capacity. Hummer: L 5507 mm; W 2201 mm; H 2055 mm.

The MG U9 BEV includes a fold-down rear bulkhead to extend cargo-bed length inside the cabin, plus a 1,200 L tub capacity and a 236 L front trunk (frunk).

Google tells me: “The MG U9 EV Dual-Cab Ute comes equipped from the factory with a four-wheel electronic air suspension system offering over 100 mm of vertical height adjustment (including an Off-Road Lift mode, and automatic lowering by -20 mm at 80 km/h and -40 mm at 120 km/h.”

Of course there is a lot more to know, especially about tech and safety features. I am just whetting your appetite. You can find out more here. Or watch a video review on YouTube here.

At the moment, the dealership is fielding 8 enquiries a week online, but it is expecting some headwinds in mid 2027 when the Australian federal government is planning to review Fringe Benefit Tax benefits for electric vehicles. My comment was that somehow car manufacturers seem to find ways of reducing costs when government support is modified.

While I peppered the sales rep with questions, Majella did her usual photographic exploration and analysis.

She was impressed by the massive frunk, as was I — you could fit a whole body in there! The frunk can be opened from the outside by a physical button.

… and it has a power point for your fridge or coffeemaker!

The ute features doors with physical buttons and handles for ease of access.

The gear lever reminded Majella of the ones we see in the cockpit of a plane.

A variety of charging options for your tech are provided: cigarette lighter, USB charger, and more.

Perhaps MG went overboard with buttons — lots of physical buttons on the dash and on the steering wheel, plus the screen. Majella thought that this was a comfortable wheel size and shape for a long drive.

Behind the back seats in a cubby hole we have the charge cords: a trickle charger and type 2 to type 2.

The back tailgate has a discreet button to push to fold it down so you can load the tray.

An emergency glass breaker and a seat belt cutter is provided.

Although MG appears to have thought of everything, Majella commented that the only thing they don’t supply is a first aid kit.

The air suspension is easily adjusted air suspension from the centre screen. The sales rep confided that the staff were playing with it the day before.

The 360 degree camera extends to the trailer, with an aerial view for easily reversing.

Those looking for a truck to haul stuff, power their tools, and store excess energy would do well to consider the MG U9 EV. It may not be a Hummer, but it’s close. For tradies and bush bashers, the future can now be bright and electric.

Maingear's revamped Zero range is already selling out, and I can see why

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Get the biggest gaming news, reviews, and releases straight to your inbox.

Join the GamesRadar community for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The revamped version of the Maingear Zero feels even more bespoke to its cable-less design philosophy. With a homemade case and cooler from Maingear itself, the Mk.2 doubles down on what made this PC great to begin with. It's a gaming desktop that emphasises this brand's pristine build quality, and one that will be an absolute showstopper to have in your setup. It comes at a premium though, and not just because of the RAMpocalypse.

Harder to upgrade if you aren't well-versed in back-connect motherboards

Why you can trust GamesRadar+ Our experts review games, movies and tech over countless hours, so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about our reviews policy.

2026 is a dreadful time to be shopping for a new desktop PC. I know that; you probably all know that, and PC hardware brands who are struggling to shift any new products due to massive price inflations definitely know that. For many, the current economy means upgrading is written off entirely, because the expense is just too high at the moment. But when the going gets tough, the tough get going, and Maingear's latest attempt to reinstate itself as the best gaming PC builder embodies that.

It seems to me that Maingear understands the following; if gamers are going to upgrade their PC at the moment, it might as well be for a good reason. In other words, the products on offer need to be flawless to entice people to buy them because they really need to be worth the added expense they bring. When I reviewed the first rendition of the Maingear Zero Ruby back in 2024, I already thought it was worth its premium price tag, not just because the computer itself was beautiful, but because this brand makes a point of going the extra mile with its products on behalf of its customers.

Back then, the Zero's concept was novel - it was the first range of prebuilt PCs to use back-connect motherboards. But at some point in 2025, the Zero range quietly disappeared from Maingear's store, only seeing a revival this year alongside the revamped MG-1 flagship the brand prides itself on. This Mk.2 rendition of the Zero is going to cost you more than the original ever did, but it makes modern enhancements that would tempt any PC gamer into ignoring the RAMpocalypse and slapping their money down on the table.

I mean, just look at this thing. The panoramic glass, the multi-angle AIO cooler display, the seven pre-installed fans, the absolutely spotless build quality? It takes the novel idea of a PC with zero visible cables we saw two years ago and takes it to the next level.

Case in point, this PC was actually really difficult to take review photos of because its fancy, futuristic glass meant no matter the angle I snapped from, I'd be somewhere in the reflection. That's an annoyance for me, but a very, very good thing for you because this is the sort of PC that displays your components beautifully, and something you'll want on display as the heart of your gaming setup. Don't put this thing under your desk - it's something to show off.

I went to Computex this year; I've seen some innovations in fans, I've seen new case designs that look fancy and futuristic, and I've seen mice with upward-firing fans in them. But I think the Zero Mk.2 might be the best-looking bit of gaming PC hardware I've seen this year. The adoption of panoramic glass is huge, but the way it works in tandem with a cooler that's visible from every angle takes the clean, cableless design philosophy to new heights. It also manages to look bigger on the inside than it actually is, but more on that in a minute.

First, we have to talk about the build quality - which I shouldn't be surprised by, knowing Maingear as a brand, but I still was. There are often nitpicks I have with prebuilt gaming PCs when I review them. For example, the GPU bracket Quoted Tech chose in the PC they sent was blatantly wrong, causing friction with an intake fan and making an annoying whirring sound. Especially having built and rebuilt my own PCs for years now, these things can be easy to spot. But I went through the Maingear Zero Mk.2 with a fine-toothed comb; there's nothing to pick apart. In the box, there's even a checklist to evidence the PC you've received has been built to high standards and stress-tested before it even gets to you. This time around, I even saw improvements on the first Zero.

Hardware nerds unite, sign up to our free tech newsletter for a weekly digest of the hottest new tech, the latest gadgets on the test bench, and much more from the team at GamesRadar+.

The annoying GPU support bracket from the original was so integrated into the motherboard that even to add a new SSD in, you had to disassemble the entire graphics card and faceplate first. This has been improved so it's more practical this time around.

Something else that I love is that Maingear has opted for a pretty reasonably sized chassis for the revamped Zero. As far as I'm aware, it's Maingear's own, homemade Zero Mk.2 case, which wasn't the case last time, as MSI's MAG Pano M100 PZ was borrowed for it - presumably as part of whatever deal was struck to get hold of the brand's motherboards.

This time around, Maingear has taken a lot of design notes from Cooler Master, MSI, Asus, and now NZXT with their curved-glass PC cases. But the actual physical size of this chassis is a compact mATX that fits ATX boards. It means the PC can be a reasonable size that'll fit on top of your desk, in a showy position in your room, or in a tighter space in a smaller gaming room.

When the first batch of Zero PCs hit the shelves, these motherboards were still relatively new on the scene; they were quirky and were harder to come by. Now, their concept isn't so novel, and there's a larger range of boards, including smaller mATX ones that help Maingear to keep things compact here.

Of course, one downside in grabbing a PC like this with a back-connect motherboard is that maintaining that pristine look over the years might make things difficult. If you want to upgrade one of these rigs but you're not that sure of how to perform various PC surgeries, the advice you find online will only be harder to follow if you take on a motherboard that isn't conventional. Furthermore, it's going to be very difficult not to disrupt Maingear's very neat and tidy PC assembly job when you upgrade, so it feels like there would be a risk of messing things up if you go to upgrade your machine later. That's just something to think about.

As you can see, the ceiling for the new range of Zero PCs is a bit ludicrous, with the Amethyst configuration (which is the closest comparison to my review unit) costing $7,299 at the time of writing. Yes, you did read that right.

Some interesting disparities between the version I tested and what you'll get are that, while I had a B860 chipset for an AMD processor, you can have either an AMD CPU for gaming or an Intel Core Ultra 200S Plus one - this is one of the few pre-built PCs outside of custom builds that allow you to configure either an AMD or Intel chipset.

Also interesting is that I had XPG RAM, while all the other versions of the Zero Mk.2 are advertised to have Kingston Fury memory in them. I don't imagine the reason I was sent something different was anything to do with hiding the performance you'll get - it's probably much more likely that Maingear was using whatever it had stock of for my temporary review unit.

Alongside this top-of-the-range configuration though, the Zero Mk.2 comes in an "Elite" version harnessing an RTX 5080. Right now, all of the named, prebuilt variations of the new Ruby are sold out, but you can still fully customize one to be built to your exact specifications. From what I gather on Maingear's builder, the Zero starts at $2,988, with minimum component options being an 8GB RTX 5060 Ti, AMD Ryzen 5 9600X, 32GB of RAM, and 2TB SSD.

If you do want to spare yourself some expense, Maingear does have a BYO-RAM option which might help to take that minimum spend down a touch. Funnily enough, I remember criticising the configurations of the original Maingear Zero for so many of them only offering 16GB of DDR5 despite their high prices. Now, during RAMageddon, the minimum this sequel PC comes with is 32GB.

As is always the case when I'm supplied with a review unit that spares absolutely no expense when it comes to power, these performance figures are going to be relevant to you only if you opt for a 5090 configuration. Do not expect these kinds of numbers if you opt for the more affordable 5060 Ti versions of this machine. Still, I'm always going to put a review model through its paces, and I was particularly keen to see if aesthetically flawless building amounted to strong performance.

The GPU being an RTX 5090, I can happily say that no PSU cables spontaneously combusted during this review - I'd love to say this is a solid sign that thermals are being handled well by the new Maingear Zero Mk.2 case, but that may just be luck of the draw with the GPU, PSU, and its cables.

On that note, the GPU was actually warmer during my testing than the CPU, with the Ryzen 7 only peaking at 69 degrees Celsius during 3DMark's Time Spy benchmark, while the RTX 5090 reached 73 degrees. Elsewhere in benchmarking the CPU had higher peaks, but the GPU had higher mean temperatures overall, which tells you a lot about the 5090. At the same time, credit where it's due goes to the Maingear 360mm AIO cooler, which kept the beefy X3D processor cool under strenuous workloads.

Cinebench:Multi Thread: 5605ptsSingle Core: 761ptsSingle Thread: 562pts

3DMark:TimeSpy: 36,896Firestrike: 66,029Steel Nomad: 140.94fps

CrystalDiskMark:Sequential read: 7,387 MB/sSequential write: 6,769 MB/s

AIDA64 Extreme / RAM readings:59,228 MB/s read30-36-36-76 (6,000MT/s)

One thing I'd like to point out is that, just like the original Maingear Zero, something that really stands out about this brand's computers is that they buck the trend of most PC builders by supplying some really top-rate RAM and SSDs. Usually, this is where pre-build companies make a bit of money back because they'll give you the headline GPU and CPU specs you want, but the quality of the RAM and SSDs will be lesser because they don't matter quite so much. Maingear's attitude is a little different, because it wants to give you nothing but the best parts, and it shows in testing.

The SSD here achieved a 7,000+MB/s read speed, which is what you'd hope for when it comes to a Gen 4 NVMe M.2 SSD - a lot of the time PCs can house SSDs that get stuck in the 5,000s, which is okay, but not great for the money you pay for one of these Zero Mk.2 rigs. At a time like this, when SSDs and RAM will be harder to come by and cost you more, that could be a good or a bad thing, but at least you know you're getting quality parts here - perhaps more affordably than if you were to buy separately due to Maingear likely buying in bulk and having partnerships with suppliers like TeamGroup.

I'd love to applaud the fact that the RAM aboard the Zero Mk.2 achieved a higher AIDA Extreme reading than desktops I've tested previously, but since it's not the standard memory from Kingston you'll receive in your machine, it may not matter so much. If you choose to bring your own RAM to the Zero Mk.2, the benchmarks above will at least show you what's possible.

It's no surprise at all that, seeing as this is actually the first 5090 rig I've reviewed so far this generation, the gaming and software benchmarks are the highest you'll see in any chart.

The RTX 5090 is a ridiculous piece of kit, but even before the RAMpocalypse, it was always priced out of reach of the majority of gamers. Even to begin with, I don't think the RTX 5090 was really designed for gamers anyway, and it was mainly designed, as the 4090 was, for AI users, crypto mining, pro-level 3D artists, and content creators. Keep that in mind while looking at the benchmarks here. They're absurdly powerful, but probably not what most gamers will actually need when buying even a premium-level PC, especially when one like this will currently cost you more than a car.

There's a hell of a combination of parts coming together to work in tandem in these results, and like I say, there's no surprise the Zero Mk.2 has recorded the highest results of any PC I've ever benchmarked.

It'd be easy for me to draw the line there and say, since it's the most powerful PC, it's the best overall, but that'd actually be cutting the Maingear Zero Mk.2 short.

There's more to actually using this PC than its parts that I think makes it the best, and after all, you might not have one with an RTX 5090 in it.

For example, thermals are really solid here, which can sometimes suffer in cases designed specifically to look good. Even under these stressful benchmarks, I don't think I really noticed the fans kicking up much of an audible fuss from under my desk once. The Zero Mk.2 is compact, it's quiet, and it's the most powerful and best-built PC I've come across. Again, the RTX 5090 configuration outguns even some of the other highest-end PCs I've benchmarked, but even with a 5080, I think the stronger supporting components here would mean the Zero Mk.2 could stand a chance at pulling ahead of the competition on performance.

Moreover, while you pay a premium for Maingear goods, you do get some of the best warranty, servicing, and customer service options I've seen from a PC building brand. Maingear PCs get lifetime service and repairs. Even if you pass the PC on to a friend or sell it on, the label and number on the back sticker of the PC means you can quote it to a service agent, and they'll be happy to help with whatever is wrong with the rig.

And the PC that arrives with you has absolutely minimal bloatware other than what Windows insists you have. The sample I received was packaged well with an inner and outer box, expanding foam in case you need to send it back or pack it up during a house move; there are set-up instructions, and everything in the PC comes properly installed so that you don't need to do a thing to get going besides plug things in and get started.

Are this company's PC's constantly in high demand, and do they cost a premium? Yes, and that might be your biggest barrier to entry.

The Maingear Zero Mk.2 has doubled down on what made the original my favorite gaming PC I've tested. It's somehow got an even cleaner build this time around, and its case and cooler only complement that, with a design that lets the cableless beauty within speak for itself.

Maingear has managed to design and execute something that lets you have the best of every world when it comes to buying a new PC. There's a compact size here that makes it versatile for your setup and the way you want to use it. There's an option to cram it with some of the beefiest and best components out there, and it's all something that looks a million dollars without making itself heard due to poor thermals.

That said, I do think this brand should be treated like the holy grail of the prebuilt market. The prices here are high, and yes, you're paying more than you would from other companies for an additional level of quality. But I wouldn't be doing my job if I didn't say there are other PC makers out there that won't charge quite as much and still give you the specs, an attractive design, and great thermals.

I think the people who should be looking for a Maingear Zero Mk.2 are the ones who want an absolute showstopper of a PC that can do it all. For those prepared to spare no expense, there's no PC I'd recommend harder.

I put the Maingear Zero Mk.2 through its paces by using it for around 2 weeks. In that time, I put it through all of the formal benchmarking tests we here at GamesRadar+ use to recommend you new computing tech. I played games with it, I created some video content, and I put it through strict benchmarking tests in both the gaming and content creation software worlds. I kept track of data on frame rates, temperatures, and more.

I also evaluated pricing, tech specs, and Maingear as a brand to come to a verdict.

For more on how we test gaming PCs, take a look at the GamesRadar+ hardware policy.

TelevisaUnivision Ups Alyssa Bernstein To Head Of Corporate Communications

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

TelevisaUnivision has promoted Alyssa Bernstein to Head of Corporate Communications.

She is taking on the top role in a restructuring that followed the exit of former Chief Communications Officer Oscar Suris last August.

Bernstein joined the Hispanic media giant in 2022 after the completion of Univision’s merger with Grupo Televisa’s media arm. A year later, she was upped to SVP of corporate communications.

Prior to TelevisaUnivision, Bernstein worked in communications for Paramount, CBS and ABC News. Related Stories Executives Kira Goldberg Leaving Netflix As VP Film To Join Paramount As President Of Motion Picture Group Alongside Don Granger; WB's Jesse Ehrman Upped News AI-Focused Management Firm Collage Adds Kelly Miller And Jack Styne As Partners

At TelevisaUnivision, Bernstein’s purview has included media relations, executive communications, financial communications and communications for U.S. ad sales and global distribution. In addition to serving as the primary spokesperson for the company, her new role will give her oversight of corporate branding and social media. Watch on Deadline

With operations in both the U.S. and Mexico, TelevisaUnivision remains privately held, but the company still conducts quarterly earnings calls with Wall Street analysts after releasing its financial results. In the third quarter, the company reported two very different sets of numbers for its U.S. and Mexico divisions, reflecting the latter’s rights to the FIFA Men’s World Cup. Along with strong ratings and advertising in Mexico, the rights generated additional revenue from sublicensing deals in other Spanish-language territories.

In her new post, Bernstein will report to Karina Awad, chief human resources officer of TelevisaUnivision, and continue to work closely with CEO Daniel Alegre, a Google veteran who took over the top job in 2024.

Content publicity, run by Ruben Acosta, as well as social impact and internal communications remain separate functions under Awad.

“We’re thrilled to have Alyssa take on this expanded role and lead a more integrated, business-focused function that strengthens our market positioning, supports growth and enhances our corporate reputation,” Awad said.

Even our bunch of diehard PC gamers aren't that bothered by 30 fps in GTA 6

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The game wears it surprisingly well, though we'll be less happy if the PC version is similarly capped.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

For a bunch of performance snobs, we are a surprisingly forgiving bunch at PC Gamer. Yes, if you try to insult XCOM, you're going to get a slap, but we've only just started playing GTA 6 (well, some of us) and we're mostly over the whole 'only runs at 30 fps' thing already.

It turns out, while we're happy to part with our money to buy expensive components to hit higher frame rates (and earn the right to brag about it), we're also just as happy to settle for less when a game does a lot of other things exceptionally well.

GTA 6 certainly does that. That's the impression I get from most members of the team that have played it so far. Though if Rockstar is listening, when you do get around to our precious PC version, we'd love nothing more than 4K60—maybe more.

I will no doubt appreciate frames above 100 fps when GTA 6 comes to PC. Heck, I'd even just appreciate going up to 60 fps. But in my handful of hours with the game thus far, I just have simply not been that bothered by it. Be it smart ways of masking low frames, or even just the slower, methodical control approach of the game, I'm less frustrated with my PS5 running the game at around 30 fps than I was recently playing Phantom Blade Zero at well above 40 fps on a handheld PC.

Maybe that's down to the world having a little bit of magic to it, maybe it's the engrossing tone and atmosphere, maybe it's just because it looks so darn pretty. Maybe it's because I haven't got to the game's most hectic moments yet and haven't really needed to hit a headshot with my console unable to keep up with me. I'll tell you how I'm getting on in another fifty hours. Cool, see you in three days, then?

I was dreading playing GTA 6 on my OG PS5—a dusty ornament that's been chilling out in my living room this last year doing absolutely nothing. I've got a nice gaming PC in my office (RTX 4090, Intel i9 13900K, 32 GB RAM), so it's been a long time since I've had to deal with games dipping below 80 fps. I have been spoiled.

The second I got to control Lucia, my fears seemed justified. Everything just felt sluggish. But that feeling didn't last. I played GTA 6 for around 5 hours last night (I am very tired) and I haven't thought about the frame rate once since that initial section. Whether I'm getting into shootouts with coke smugglers, speeding through the Keys at night, or bouncing around at sea, GTA 6 looks great and feels great.

I can still tell this isn't 60 fps, but it is smooth.

I can still tell this isn't 60 fps, but it is smooth. I'm sure I've encountered drops, but crucially I haven't noticed them. Sure, it's inevitably going to look better and run better on PC, but the lower performance on my PS5 quickly ceased to be an issue, and I'm not regretting playing it on console.

GTA 6 is the kind of game where an uncapped framerate would be nice, but it doesn't feel essential. Like Red Dead Redemption 2 before it—a game I also played at 30 fps before it launched on PC—its pacing is measured. It feels designed around the limitations of the consoles it's launching on, prioritising detail and depth over twitch-based combat and precise interaction. It's exactly what I'd expect from a modern Rockstar game. Also like Red Dead Redemption 2 before it, I still feel the sense of longing for what this game will look like on PC. Make no mistake: the version currently available is a preview for what GTA 6 will be at its best. It's great, but those who can wait will be treated to a superior experience.

It's also possible that the 30 fps cap will feel more restrictive once the online mode arrives—whenever that will be, and in whatever form it takes. It's one thing to handle a lower framerate within Rockstar's meticulously created campaign. It's quite another in a lobby of other players. If GTA 6's multiplayer is even half as chaotic as the current GTA Online, every frame is going to count.

As I was booting up GTA 6, I was expecting to be annoyed by the game's paltry frame rate. And I was—at first. Much like Fraser, during the opening scene with Lucia, I saw my fears come to life on the big screen in front of me. Objects juddering and blurring as I swung my camera around in the cramped cell. Did I just waste £80 on GTA 6 on PS5 when I should've held my nerve for the PC version?

Truth is, the frame rate has had nowhere near as much of a negative impact on my experience while I've been traversing the Floridian wilds as Jason. In part, I think that's because there's a lot more going on, as cars, shops, people, and poultry fly past me as I break most state, federal and ethical laws. That reduces the chance of my eyes fixating on any one object and noticing the adverse effects of the game's lower fps, or the ways Rockstar has gone about covering for it.

Low fps is one thing, but motion blur is the devil.

Low fps is one thing, but motion blur is the devil. That's not just my opinion, that's been scientifically verified (sorta) to be the opinion of PC Gamer readers. Admittedly, some motion blur may be required when the frame rate is this low, so I have played around with a few settings in GTA 6 to find the perfect balance. I'd heard through the grapevine that higher-than-default motion blur (default is 3, goes up to 10) may be preferential to reduce the more obvious juddering that is present at low fps, but whoever says that is no friend of mine. I've landed on setting motion blur to 1 on the standard PS5, as it feels the cleanest overall. I wouldn't blame anyone for turning it off entirely, especially on a more powerful console where the frame rate may not dip as much.

Of course, I do agree with the rest of this lot: in an ideal world, playing the PC version, I'd be pushing a much higher frame rate with motion blur banished to the abyss where it belongs, and a full selection of graphics settings to tinker with to my liking. Yet, to my surprise, I'm not as much of a console version curmudgeon as I thought I'd be.

Is the low frame rate noticeable? Yep. Would it be better at a higher frame rate? Also yep. Is the low frame rate a detriment to my overall enjoyment of GTA 6? Only a smidge.

I don't consider myself much of a framerate snob, but I'll admit I was side-eyeing GTA 6's 30fps just a little bit. Well, that was before the game came out. Now that I have my hands on it, with roughly six hours spent lollygagging around Leonida? I'm hardly noticing it, though I'll most definitely be appreciating a smoother experience once the PC version rolls around.

Like Jacob said, motion blur definitely seems to be masking it somewhat—and I am traditionally a motion blur hater, so it's taken a little getting used to—but I'm genuinely pleasantly surprised at the balance Rockstar has managed to strike between a smooth performance and a dense, rich world.

GTA 6 taught me that I don't much care about frame rates as much as I expected. I got to start playing it a few weeks ago and didn't have anyone to compare notes with, and my first thought was: Huh, they got it to run at 60 fps after all! It all felt very smooth, even when I realized it was 30 fps after all. It was only a bit later I started noticing very occasional drops, like while driving during a gradual lighting chance from day to night.

But that's really infrequent. I think it being consistent is key. I'm on a PS5 Slim, by the way, not a Pro. That's not to say I still can't wait to see it at higher frame rates when it finally gets to PC. But 30 fps feels mostly fine for the time being.

It was actually spending a lot of time playing with handheld gaming PCs and laptops with integrated graphics that reminded me I shouldn't give as much of a shit about frame rate as I do. Largely that obsession is born of having high-end hardware sat in my desktop PC, but when crashing around with a range of handhelds I'm reminded of the old days when 30 fps was a target for PC games, too.

It's also reminded me that it really depends on the game engine, as well. Not every 30 fps game looks the same as other 30 fps games. And Rockstar has obviously worked incredibly hard to make sure that 30 fps looks as good as it possible can. Otherwise why would that be a solid target for all four of the console platforms it's released on?

For the most part Rockstar has done a great job of keeping things smooth.

I've been playing GTA 6 on the Xbox Series X, which does suffer from the odd dip in frame rate, too—though oddly I've only really noticed it during canned animations the characters do starting certain actions, such as opening the trunk of a car or pulling something out of it. But for the most part Rockstar has done a great job of keeping things smooth. And not just by using motion blur. For the record, I'm running that at 0 and have not looked back.

I'm less happy about the vignetting that I'm getting around the darkened edges of the screen, but I'm playing it blown up on a 4K projector so I've got plenty of screen real estate to enjoy. And I have been, thoroughly enjoying it, I mean. The frame rate, for me, hasn't got in the way once.

2. Best budget 4K: Asus ROG Strix XG27UCG3. Best budget OLEDAlienware AW2726DM

Hashi Mainnet Goes Live With 20+ Partners, Bringing Bitcoin Finance to Sui at Scale

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The below article is sponsored, and the views in it do not represent those of ZyCrypto. Readers should conduct independent research before taking any actions related to the project mentioned in this piece. This article should not be regarded as investment advice.

Hashi, Sui’s native Bitcoin finance infrastructure, is now live on mainnet — with more than 20 custody, retail, and DeFi participants on board, forming one of the broadest coalitions in Bitcoin finance to date.

Bitcoin has spent years becoming one of the world’s largest pools of capital. Now the infrastructure to put it to work has arrived. Supporting institutional and retail access are names including Anchorage Digital, Fluid, HoneyB, and Upshift — with BitGo, Inc. expected to follow in 2027.

The ecosystem continues to expand. Among the latest to join Hashi are Merkl, an onchain incentive platform, and Current, a lending protocol — additions that broaden the range of DeFi products and services being built around Bitcoin on Sui.

Hashi keeps Bitcoin on the Bitcoin network and mints hBTC — a 1:1 Bitcoin-backed asset — on Sui. That hBTC can then serve as collateral for lending, borrowing, and other onchain financial products.

Crucially, Hashi is decentralized by design. It’s secured by validators rather than any single entity, using industry-leading cryptography. On that foundation, it connects leading providers across custody, trading, and decentralized finance — giving Bitcoin holders a wide range of ways to put their assets to work.

With hBTC now live, Bitcoin holders have a new set of opportunities within reach — starting with borrowing against BTC and tapping new financial strategies through lending markets and vaults.

To meet the specific needs of institutions, several new providers are rolling out dedicated lending and vault facilities. Fluid’s institutional-grade lending protocol is now live on Sui, beginning with Bitcoin-backed stablecoin loans. Holders can use Hashi to bring BTC onto Sui as hBTC, then post it as collateral on Fluid to borrow stablecoins — starting with USDC, with more flows to come.

“Bitcoin is the largest pool of idle capital in crypto,” said Samyak Jain, founder and CEO at Fluid. “With Hashi, it can now power deep, capital-efficient credit markets on Fluid.”

For institutions, Hashi solves a key piece of the puzzle: custody. Institutions will be able to access Hashi through two leading crypto custodians — Anchorage Digital and BitGo, Inc. (“BitGo”) — giving them a clear path to hold or deploy BTC into Hashi.

Previously announced institutional partners, including Cumberland and Wave Digital Assets, will play roles as early participants — deploying BTC and stablecoins on Hashi and providing liquidity across a range of strategies on Sui.

On the DeFi side, Sui protocols including Blueprint Finance (Concrete), Bluefin, Cetus, Current, and Suilend will unlock new lending opportunities, stablecoin credit, and yield strategies — all backed by Bitcoin.

“Some of the world’s largest Bitcoin holders are sitting on enormous amounts of untapped financial potential,” said Nathan McCauley, CEO and Co-Founder at Anchorage Digital. “Hashi gives our institutional clients a new way to access Bitcoin finance on Sui, with their BTC held in custody at Anchorage Digital Bank.”

If you’re an institution interested in deploying BTC or stablecoins on Hashi, please reach out to your custodian or hashi@sui.io. Users can explore more at sui.io/hashi.

A New Fallout Game Leaks, But Dogmeat Won’t Like It

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Daniel is a lifelong gamer with a love for various genres and gameplay styles who enjoys exploring games and playing from various angles.

Here’s a fun fact: in the lore of the Fallout series, dogs weren’t allowed into the Vault-Tec Vaults, with all the dogs we see throughout the games being those that survived and evolved into wolf-like forms after the Great War. Cats, on the other hand, were permitted into the Vaults within reason thanks to their applications as therapy pets and efficacy as pest-hunters. In spite of that, cats didn’t start appearing in the Fallout games until Fallout 4 and Fallout 76. Apparently, the reason for that is that they all just sorta got lost somewhere.

A newly-discovered document from the Australian Classification Board has leaked the existence of a new Fallout spin-off game titled A Wasteland Full of Cats. This game is apparently being created in cooperation between Bethesda and Devcats, an indie developer specializing in hidden object games about cats like A Park Full of Cats, A Building Full of Cats, and An Arcade Full of Cats.

Is it the Fallout game we’ve all been waiting for in the near-decade since the launch of Fallout 76? No, definitely not, but, well, it’s something, I suppose. Every bit of Fallout-adjacent content we get can continue to tide us over until Fallout 5, whenever that ends up being, or at least the next season of the Fallout television series.

Tim Cain, the creator of Fallout, initially proposed a much happier ending, but it was ultimately scrapped.

According to the description of the game on the Australian Classification Board website, A Wasteland Full of Cats will cast you in the role of one of the various cats living in one of the Vaults that dot the Wasteland. Following some manner of accident, all of the other cats living in the Vault alongside you are let loose out into the Wasteland, and you’ll need to go out there to round them all up.

In the same vein as Devcats’s other games, this will be a jaunt through 14 locations and 33 rooms where you’ll need to point out the errant cats hidden within the scenery, Where’s Waldo-style.

A Wasteland Full of Cats will cast you in the role of one of the various cats living in one of the Vaults.

You’ll be helped along the way by various NPC characters, hopefully including some familiar faces, with many of the locations you visit apparently being classic spots from across the Fallout franchise. Some of these locations will have mild puzzle elements, allowing you to uncover non-critical secret levels. According to the description, the game will culminate in a final battle against a horde of Radroaches, though I’m admittedly not quite sure how finding cats translates into battling giant mutant roaches, but I guess we’ll find out.

It has been an exceptionally hot minute since the last mainline Fallout game, specifically the release of Fallout 4 in 2015. Since then, it’s been pretty much exclusively spin-offs, such as the mobile title Fallout Shelter, its gacha-added version, Fallout Shelter Online, the MMO stylings of Fallout 76, and I guess Zen Pinball’s Fallout table for the Pinball FX games, if you want to count that.

Back in 2022, Todd Howard said in an interview with IGN that development on Fallout 5 would begin in earnest once The Elder Scrolls VI was done and dusted. Of course, considering we only just learned that game’s full title recently, I have a sinking feeling the timeline has shifted on that a bit. For what it’s worth, Bethesda did say in an X, formerly Twitter post this past July that Fallout 5 has officially begun pre-production, but again, that doesn’t give us much to go on.

As of now, we don’t have any particular release dates or even release windows for upcoming Fallout projects, whether it’s mainline games, the TV show, or even this new cat thing. Just gotta keep wading through the Wasteland patiently, I guess…

Amazon's Fallout is a great game-to-film adaptation that both longtime fans and newcomers adore, and here's what we find it did perfectly.

Proton Pass is up to 60% off for Black Friday—and it does way more than just store your passwords

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There’s a familiar interruption that comes with shopping online: you’ve found what you want, you’re ready to check out, and the store asks you to sign in. Was this the account with your usual password? Did you change it last time? Is it easier to reset it again?

Proton Pass is designed to take that friction out of your day. It creates strong, unique passwords, remembers them for you, and fills them in when you log in. But it also handles something a password alone can’t: keeping your real email address out of another retailer’s database.

For Black Friday, Proton Pass Plus and Pass Family are both 60% off their regular monthly pricing with a one-year subscription, while Proton Unlimited is 50% off. Pass Plus starts at $1.99 per month, billed annually, making this a good opportunity to get your passwords — and a few other important parts of your digital life — organized.

The problem with strong passwords isn’t understanding why you need them; it’s keeping track of a different one for every account.

Proton Pass does that work for you. When you sign up for a website, its password generator can create a strong password that’s completely unique. Save it in your vault, and Proton Pass can fill in your credentials when you return.

That’s useful whether you’re creating an account for a single purchase or signing into a service you use every day.

Unique passwords also help limit the fallout from a data breach. If a retailer exposes your login credentials, using a different password elsewhere means those same credentials won’t unlock your other accounts.

With unlimited logins and encrypted vaults that sync across devices, Proton Pass lets you keep those details together as you move between your phone and computer.

Proton Pass is available for practically every platform, from Chrome and Firefox on the web to native macOS, Windows, and Linux apps to, of course, iOS and Android. This makes it trivially easy to access your passwords, passkeys, and API credentials wherever you find yourself.

Black Friday shopping often leaves something behind long after the packages arrive: a steady stream of promotional emails from stores you barely remember visiting.

Proton Pass includes unlimited hide-my-email aliases, giving you an alternative to handing out your real address every time you create an account.

An alias is an extra email address that forwards messages to your normal inbox. You can give it to a shopping website and still receive your order confirmations and delivery updates, without sharing the underlying address you use for everything else.

If that alias starts receiving spam, you can switch it off. Your real email address stays the same, and you don’t have to update all your other accounts.

It’s a practical way to put some distance between your primary inbox and the websites you’re trying for the first time. A separate alias for each service also gives you more control: you can stop messages arriving through one address without disrupting the others.

Passwords are only part of what’s worth keeping in a secure, accessible place.

Proton Pass also stores notes, credit card details, and other confidential information in end-to-end encrypted vaults. That makes it useful for the small, everyday moments when you need something sensitive and don’t want to go looking for it.

When you’re booking a flight, saved passport details let you look up the information you need without digging through a drawer halfway through the reservation.

When you’re shopping online, saved card details mean you don’t have to leave the checkout page to find your wallet.

When you’re traveling, keeping a digital copy of an important document within reach gives you a convenient reference while you’re away from home. It doesn’t replace an original document where one is required, but it can save you from searching through scattered files and messages.

Pass Plus also includes a built-in two-factor authenticator, Dark Web Monitoring, and secure link and vault sharing. Those features give you more ways to manage access to your accounts and share sensitive information when you need to.

Pass Plus is the straightforward choice for one person who wants password generation, autofill, unlimited aliases, and secure storage.

Pass Family includes six Pass Plus accounts and a family admin panel. If several people in your household need a password manager, it’s worth considering rather than setting everyone up with separate subscriptions.

Proton Unlimited bundles Pass with Proton VPN, Proton Mail, Proton Drive, and more, including 500 GB of cloud storage. It’s the option to consider if you also want a VPN, encrypted email, and cloud storage alongside your password manager.

A password manager isn’t the most conspicuous Black Friday purchase, but it’s one you can put to work immediately: the next account you create, the next password you forget, or the next time a checkout asks for details you don’t have handy.

Proton’s offers are available to new users and include a 30-day money-back guarantee.

CoreWeave co-founder bought $30 million of the company's bonds to hold

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The unsecured bonds mature in October 2031, and the company is seeking at least $4 billion through an investment-grade loan

CoreWeave co-founder Brian Venturo spent $30 million on debt issued by the AI cloud firm and intends to keep it until it comes due, according to Bloomberg.

Venturo holds the title of chief strategy officer at CoreWeave, and he picked up the unsecured debt last week, according to Bloomberg. The bonds mature in October 2031. He disclosed the purchase in a LinkedIn post.

"When we started CoreWeave, I couldn't have imagined the scale of what our customers are building with us today," Venturo wrote, according to Bloomberg. "I believe in their work, and I'm proud of the team we've built to support it. I've never been more confident in CoreWeave."

A CoreWeave representative declined to comment, according to Bloomberg. Venturo could not immediately be reached.

CoreWeave has been landing additional clients and building out its infrastructure footprint, according to Bloomberg. Its spending pace and its dependence on speculative-grade borrowing have made investors uneasy about the price tag of the AI boom. Several of its bonds dropped into territory associated with distress, then recovered modestly last week once quarterly results topped forecasts.

Bloomberg described such a move as unusual, since executives seldom put money into their employer's debt and then disclose it to the public. Regulators generally require disclosure only for equity stakes.

Another CoreWeave co-founder, Brannin McBee, shook the confidence of bondholders and shareholders last month by laying out how the firm intends to borrow, according to Bloomberg. Speaking again on Wednesday at a technology conference in Austin, McBee said he expects stronger returns from investing cash in computing capacity than from repurchasing some of the company's high-yield debt.

CoreWeave is also looking to borrow no less than $4 billion in an investment-grade loan, according to Bloomberg.

Sociable: Instagram adds Amazon affiliate links

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The partnership will enable creators to add up to 30 shoppable products per Reel or 20 per feed post.

“Sociable” is the latest commentary on important social media developments and trends from industry expert Andrew Hutchinson of Social Media Today.

Instagram has added another way for creators to make money, by partnering with Amazon to enable affiliate links for Amazon-listed products in Reels and feed posts.

As shown in these example images, creators can select from a range of products on Amazon to add to their posts. Clickable links to the relevant product listings on Amazon will then be added in the bottom left of a post or Reel display, allowing viewers to easily make a purchase.

All affiliate links will be connected to the creator’s account, and creators will earn a commission for any sales generated from those referrals.

Eligible creators can link their Instagram and Amazon Influencer accounts in their Professional Dashboard on IG in order to facilitate product linking.

As per an Instagram press release sent to SMT: “Once connected, Instagram automatically includes their affiliate links when they add Amazon products to content, with product links taking viewers directly to the Amazon Shopping App.”

Instagram also said creators will be able to add up to 30 products per Reel, or 20 per feed post. This should provide a heap of linking potential for affiliate opportunities.

Creators can also see the commission rate for each item, so they’ll know how much they’ll get from any resulting link clicks.

This is another way for Instagram to provide monetization pathways for its creators, while also giving Amazon another means to promote its products via creator endorsement.

Amazon has been steadily expanding its product display opportunities, with the shopping giant also inking similar affiliate partnerships with YouTube and Pinterest this year.

Though, interestingly, Amazon asked to be removed from the list of platforms that will work with Meta’s Muse agent Amazon said that was because the Muse system was in violation of Amazon’s automated shopping tool restrictions.

A partnership with Instagram, then, seems like an interesting development, which could also lead to Amazon eventually working with Meta’s other platforms more openly.

Lightroom Alternative ACDSee Photo Studio Is Leaning Heavier Into AI

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Lightroom Alternative ACDSee Photo Studio Is Leaning Heavier Into AI

ACDSee Photo Studio has announced version 27, which brings a bunch of new features, including several powered by AI, perhaps elevating the tool to the level of Adobe’s offerings.

Version 27 of the photo management and editing software for macOS introduces generative AI tools for creating and editing images, along with AI-powered organization that the company says can find and group photos together by how they look. These new options don’t compromise non-destructive editing, either.

In short, ACDSee has embraced AI editing.

“AI is shaping the future of photography and it’s our goal to lead the way in that space,” Frank Lin, CTO of ACD Systems, says. “With ACDSee Photo Studio for Mac 27, we’re bringing generative AI directly into the creative process, making it easier to find, organize and transform images while giving photographers the flexibility and control they need to bring their vision to life.”

The bulk of the updates to version 27 are AI-focused, with the top-billed new feature having the highest chance of being the most controversial: image generation.

“Turn a text prompt into an image, choose a preset visual effect, or bring in reference images to guide character, style, and composition, right inside the photo workflow,” ACDSee says. AI generation isn’t new for photography tools, as Photoshop has had it for years now, but it’s not necessarily something that photographers are specifically asking for in a Lightroom competitor. Still, it’s here now.

ACDSee appears to be following Adobe’s lead with the next feature, as it now supports editing via prompting. Photographers can describe a change in plain language or “use reference images to guide the look, and reshape a photo without a single brush stroke.”

The AI search tool looks to be an alternative to Excire Photo and will find photos visually related to any primary target, with no keywords or tagging required. Images can also be grouped by similarity.

ACDSee Photo Studio for Mac 27 is available for $119.99, including 10,000 AI Credits for generative AI tools and 100GB of cloud storage.

Ubisoft Lays Off Over 150 More Developers With No Big Releases In Sight

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Assassin's Creed Black Flag: ResyncedUbisoft

Ahead of its quarterly earnings release and without any major releases on the horizon, Ubisoft is laying off 166 of its employees in Montreal and Quebec.

This was reported today by Vertical Slice, which received confirmation from Ubisoft via the following statement:

Ubisoft is reducing its workforce by 166 positions across its MontrÃ©al and QuÃ©bec City operations. As our portfolio evolves, our production needs are changing, requiring us to adjust the size of some teams to ensure our resources are aligned with current production priorities and business needs. We deeply value the contributions of these teams and recognize the impact this may have on our colleagues.

Ubisoft has been steadily laying off swathes of employees in the last year alone, letting one in six German employees go across Ubisoft DÃ¼sseldorf, Ubisoft Berlin and Ubisoft Mainz in October, shutting down Finland’s Ubisoft Redlynx, cutting 380 staff in June alongside the closure of Ubisoft Winnipeg and Ubisoft Belgrade, and laying off 40 at Ubisoft Toronto in February, and that’s just in 2026. There were hundreds more layoffs the year prior and the year before that, as well as restructurings and studio closures, with Ubisoft opting to slowly chip away at its many, many global studios rather than just lay off thousands in one sweep as other companies have. The company is, and has been for a while, in trouble, and investors are not happy.

Even so, it’s still apparently bidding to buy Arkane off Xbox.

Right now, it’s unclear what the company’s strategy is going to be to pull things back together. Though Assassin’s Creed: Black Flag Resynced was well-received earlier this year, it’s…kind of the last massive thing Ubisoft had going for it. The company has a few games in the pipeline to come, including Rayman Legends Retold in December, Rainbow Six Tactics and Heroes of Might and Magic III Remake next year, and some mythical Assassin’s Creed games and Beyond Good and Evil 2, none of which have release dates and the latter of which has been in development hell for nearly two decades.

The company just dropped its quarterly earnings report, which showed its sales up 16.8 percent year-over-year and its net bookings down 20.8 percent. The report details no specific figures for how well any of its games did, just saying that Assassin’s Creed Black Flag Resynced “outperformed expectations.” The company claims its new operating model is “fully in place” and that its cost reduction plan is “on track,” while touting its new “Motherbrain” AI initiative. Ubisoft says that its headcount has gone down by nearly 1,100 over the last two quarters.

alt: Amazon’s last-gen Kindle Paperwhite is just $10 more than the base modelalt: The last-gen Kindle Paperwhite for $130 is a stellar Black Friday deal

Amazon debuted its latest Kindle Paperwhite in October, which is now available for $199.99 and replaces the outgoing 2024 model. However, given the minimal improvements on the new model — it’s a little thinner and opens books faster — the last-gen Paperwhite is still one of the best e-readers you can buy. And now, the latter is down to $129.99 ($70 off), nearly its lowest price ever, at Amazon, Best Buy, and Target.

Some important pricing context: The waterproof e-reader held steady at $159.99 until late August, when its price rose 25 percent due to rising component costs (many other Amazon tech products got more expensive, too). Even if the price hike never happened, though, this would still be a nice discount.

The $129.99 Paperwhite includes 16GB of storage and features lockscreen ads (it costs $20 extra to opt out at checkout), just like the newer version. It also packs a sharp 300ppi display with adjustable color temperature and USB-C charging. If you want more features — including twice the storage, zero ads, wireless charging, and an auto-adjusting front light — opt instead for the Kindle Paperwhite Signature Edition while it’s down to $179.99 (also $70 off), which is just $10 shy of its best price to date.

For the sake of being comprehensive, the last-gen base Kindle is going for $119.99 ($30 off) at Amazon ahead of Black Friday. While the standard Kindle has improved with each new iteration, it’s tough to recommend when the vastly superior Paperwhite is on sale for just $10 more. That 10 bucks gets you so much: waterproofing, better battery life, faster page-turning speeds, and a higher pixel density screen that’s slightly larger (7 inches vs. 6 inches).

Stephen Colbert Signs With UTA

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The former ‘Late Show’ host and current ‘Lord of the Rings’ writer will be represented by the Beverly Hills-based talent agency.

Stephen Colbert has signed with UTA as he explores opportunities after The Late Show concluded earlier this year, The Hollywood Reporter has learned.

UTA will be representing the 13-time Emmy winner as well as his production company, Spartina Industries, across film, television, publishing work and more.

“We are thrilled to be working with Stephen Colbert. He is an exceptionally talented artist and creator, a dedicated philanthropist and a terrific person,” UTA president of filmed entertainment Matt Rice and managing partners Jay Gassner and Julien Thuan said in a joint statement. “We look forward to partnering with Stephen and Spartina Industries to support and further expand his creative endeavors.”

Colbert signed off from The Late Show in May after hosting the late-night series, which became a casualty of the Paramount-Skydance merger, for a decade. In the process, the comedian — who also wrote on and executive produced the series — collected several of his many Emmys as well as a Peabody Award.

Prior to his tenure at The Late Show, Colbert hosted, wrote and produced Comedy Central’s satire news program The Colbert Report for nine years and wrote and served as a correspondent on The Daily Show for seven years.

And Colbert is keeping busy post-Late Show. Currently, he’s co-writing the next Lord of the Rings movie with veteran LOTR screenwriter Philippa Boyens and his son Peter McGee, as well as working on his screenwriting software company Scripto. In a recent interview with THR, Colbert said he has been entertaining other opportunities but hasn’t landed on one yet. “There’s some fun stuff to think about. I’m in no hurry,” he said.

His production company, Spartina Industries, is currently developing a TV adaptation of Roger Zelazny’s sci-fi book series The Chronicles of Amber with The Walking Dead co-creator Robert Kirkman’s Skybound Entertainment. The company, headed up by Carrie Byalick, previously produced two seasons of the late-night show After Midnight on CBS.

Colbert continues to be managed by James Baby Doll Dixon at Dixon Talent, Inc. and represented by Tom Hansen and Don Steele at Hansen, Jacobson, Teller, Hoberman.
