‘Aurora’ Short Film Team Angad Aulakh And Guy Kent On Tapping Into The Emotional Weight Of Self & Outer Space

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Angad Aulakh’s lifelong fascination with space inspired Aurora, a sci-fi short about an astronaut left stranded after a mission goes catastrophically wrong. Freddie Ballard (Guy Kent) is sent to investigate a mysterious signal, but an accident makes him the sole survivor, with no way home and growing doubts about what his superiors have told him.

Shot in black and white, the film draws on German Expressionism and retro-futurism to create its unique visual world. Aulakh and Kent talk to Deadline about shaping Freddie’s journey, building the film’s cosmic scale on a short-film budget and their plans for what lies beyond the vast reaches of the galaxy they created. Related Stories News Actor Russell Tovey Dismantles The Manosphere In His Award-Winning Performance In 'I Am The Prize' News 'Vultures' Swoops To Top Award At Oscar-Qualifying HollyShorts Film Festival: Winners List

DEADLINE: How did you come up with this concept? Watch on Deadline

ANGAD AULAKH: It really started with a lifelong obsession with space and the idea that we are not alone in the universe. It’s one of the most compelling ideas for storytellers and people in general. When we look at how short our lives are, how small Earth is and how vast the universe is, the possibilities for what’s out there are so exciting. It’s the idea that we might not even be asking the right questions yet. We don’t know which questions to ask because there are concepts so grand and profound. Exploring them through storytelling felt like a wonderful opportunity.

With short films, you have more leeway to be a little avant-garde. I often liken short films to haikus, while features are full-fledged novels. So I wanted to focus on the emotion, the moment, the visuals and the wonder of space. Technology has evolved so much that it’s why we can make a film like this on our budget today.

I’m happy to say we didn’t use any AI on the film. I’m not throwing shade at AI, by any means, but I know it’s a big part of the conversation right now. It started with looking up at the stars and wondering what’s out there and what stories I could explore in the vastness of space.

DEADLINE: Guy, I read that the two of you have partnered on several projects. Is this one the most ethereal? When you heard about the idea, what went through your mind?

GUY KENT: I was thrilled because I also have a lifelong fascination with space and our place in [the cosmos]. As Angad said, we have so many questions and no answers. Finding some faith that there’s meaning despite not having the answers is profound to me. When this idea came up, it was an exciting adventure to embark on.

Angad and I have been deliberate about budget and scope, especially with short films. We prioritize quality and want to understand what we can and can’t achieve. With this film, the next question was, “How are we going to pull this off? How do we make it feel like the work we want to do, while making sure it works, resonates and lets the emotional moments land?”

Our pre-production work and the team we’ve worked with in the past gave us a built-in shorthand. That was especially true with our cinematographer, but finding a stage where we could create a set and build on it was also a huge part of making this feasible.

DEADLINE: The short film rests on you, Guy. You have a lot of scenes on your own. Your character is not only lost in space, but lost to himself, or somewhere in between. How did the two of you work on that story? What would you like audiences to think about through that juxtaposition?

KENT: Angad and I had a lot of discussions that began with isolation. Isolation isn’t something you show; it’s something you sit in. It’s revealing to see what happens when all distractions are removed and there’s no barrier between you and your own thoughts.

For Freddie, there’s a chance to find himself again. He’s an astronaut trained for crisis, someone who understands how to solve most of the problems he faces. Here, he’s confronted with something very personal and existential that he has no answers for.

It would be interesting if audiences were left wondering not only how they would react to meeting something beyond Earth, but also considering the hope that there’s something greater. As Angad said, even if we don’t have answers to these questions, there can be meaning in an unknowable answer. That makes you feel found in a way.

DEADLINE: Angad, what was the hardest thing about putting this story together?

AULAKH: The biggest challenge was looking at short films as a whole and thinking about how much time we had and how long the film would be. There are practical considerations, too. A lot of festivals won’t accept a short film longer than 15 minutes.

AULAKH: There are practical considerations around the film’s life after it’s released, but audiences also expect a short film to be short. Ideally, you’re not making a 30- or 40-minute short, because then you might as well make a feature. We had to think about the real estate available to us and the most effective way to make audiences leave having felt something and gone on an emotional experience. That’s what I’m interested in as a filmmaker: moving people. A big part of that is considering how the music, sound design, performance and production design work together to convey something cinematic and effective.

You also want to tell a compelling story. Weaving Freddie’s journey into a cohesive narrative in 15 minutes was always going to be a challenge, but I’m pleased with how it turned out. We focused on the emotion and on the fact that a mission has gone wrong. This astronaut is either going to be killed aboard the spacecraft or find his way home. I hoped the quieter moments would sustain the audience because they’d want to stick around to see whether Freddie makes it.

DEADLINE: Let’s talk about the scope of the film. The cinematography is great. Why black and white as your color scheme?

AULAKH: German Expressionism was on my mind as I wrote the screenplay and thought about the aesthetic I wanted. The other reason is that we don’t see many black-and-white films set in space or black-and-white science-fiction films. More than anything, I think it looks gorgeous on screen. The light on the set, shining through the top grates, was beautiful.

We had scouted locations beforehand, so we knew what the set would look like. But the first image that came to mind was Freddie Ballard lying on the floor, screaming and tumbling. I saw it in black and white. I hadn’t shot a black-and-white film since film school, and I thought this was the perfect opportunity. The stars look beautiful in black and white. There was also a practical reason. Black and white is more forgiving when you don’t have a huge budget for production design. You can hide things in the shadows, and it worked well with our special effects and the 3D modeling. We also made sequences in Unreal Engine with Stuart Lippincott, who’s an absolute genius. His work was a huge inspiration to me. I thought the lighting, images, and compositions would be tremendous in black and white.

KENT: On an emotional level, I think black and white subconsciously creates a sense of melancholy. That’s the basis of German Expressionism, at least in my experience. There’s an emotional texture it brings.

AULAKH: Yeah, it’s very melancholic. Black and white also has inherent cinematic associations. Audiences think of films from the 1930s and ’40s, most of which were made in black and white. I love the idea that Aurora can feel like a product of the past, something you might have seen in those bygone eras. That was exciting because retro-futurism, which is used extensively in science-fiction films and video games, is powerful and interesting. I gravitate toward it, so I loved being able to use that aesthetic in Aurora.

DEADLINE: What were the biggest technical challenges, and where did you have to get especially inventive? You’ve talked about using black and white, but is there another example? How did you get inventive with the production design?

AULAKH: A lot of the credit goes to our visual effects artist, Ian Mills Miller. The entity you see in the film was originally going to be an actor, Ethan [Daniel Corbett], wearing a latex bodysuit. We were going to use his movements and digitally map them onto a being, the way they did with Gollum in The Lord of the Rings. Instead, we used Ethan as a reference, and Ian created the magnificent 3D being you see in the film.

That’s a hard shot to pull off because it’s expensive and time-consuming, and you have to be careful about how much you show. Black and white allowed us to hide the entity in the shadows. He’s a being of light at the end of the film, but throughout it he looks dark and mechanical. He’s influenced more by machinery than by alien life.

It was tricky. Having the ability to hide him in the shadows and take a less-is-more approach was effective. If you hold an effects shot too long, the audience may start to look at it differently. Even in major motion pictures, it can be disconcerting and create an uncanny valley effect. We wanted to avoid that with our digital effects.

DEADLINE: Talk a bit about your experience on the film festival circuit.

AULAKH: I’ll take the first part, and Guy can talk about where we’ve been and what’s coming up. We premiered at HollyShorts this August, which was a lovely experience. I’m a small-town kid from Mountaintop, Pennsylvania. We didn’t have a movie theater in town, and we still don’t. Getting to screen a film at the historic Grauman’s Chinese Theatre was an honor.

The festival was so well put together, and the audience response was wonderful. There were events throughout the week, so you had a chance to meet other filmmakers and talk with people watching your film. As a filmmaker, you get real-time feedback from the room. Unfortunately, we can’t attend many festivals because they’re all over the world. The few opportunities we get to sit with an audience and see the film on the big screen are phenomenal. It’s been wonderful so far. Our last few shorts had great festival runs, and we hope Aurora will top both of them. We’re just getting started. Guy can tell you more about where we’re headed next.

KENT: We’re going to FilmQuest. We are nominated for Best Sci-Fi Short and Best Visual Effects, which is fantastic. We’re thrilled to be part of that festival and what it represents. They’ve been wonderful. We’ve also done Anomaly Film Festival and Toronto Arthouse, and we’re going to Festival of Fantastic Films in the U.K. We have some other festivals to be announced toward the end of the year and early next year.

The greatest thing, as Angad said, is connecting with audiences and getting the film’s message out there. I think it’s a special piece, and there’s more to this world that we hope to build beyond the short. The more audiences see it, the more we’ll be able to go down that road.

AULAKH: We’re developing a television series based on Aurora and its world. If we can build some excitement throughout the festival circuit, it’ll make getting the series off the ground that much easier. We hope people respond to the film. After the festival circuit, we’ll release it online; we’re working on distribution for that, too.

These festival runs can last upwards of a year and a half, if you’re lucky. We’re excited to get the film out there around the world, and it has officially gone international. Sometimes things can go right in life, and this is luckily one of those situations. I don’t want to knock on wood as I say that. We’re thrilled and honored to be selected.

GTA 6 On Switch 2? Digital Foundry Weighs In On The Possibility

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Could Grand Theft Auto 6 come to Switch 2? That has been the big question ever since Nintendo officially revealed its console last year, and it's something that we have mused over ever since (as unlikely as it may seem at an initial glance). Now that the game is out, the tech experts over at Digital Foundry have weighed in on the port possibility, and, perhaps unsurprisingly, they don't rate its chances.

In the outlet's latest rundown, it touches on the "compromised" Xbox Series S performance. It isn't all that, with DF noting "frame-rates routinely lingering in the mid to high 20s during ordinary traversal and combat," but it's the system that regularly lands the most comparisons to Switch 2, so DF says that it would be "interesting" to see if it would be "possible for a further cut back version to ship on Nintendo's hybrid console".

However, the experts point out that a Switch 2 port "feels extremely unlikely at this stage".

"Unless there are massive changes made," DF's John Linneman says around the 53:24 mark in the above video, "I don't see how this could possibly work on Switch 2".

The main reasoning seems to mostly come down to the sheer size of the damn thing. This is a game that is pushing even the highest-end consoles to their limit — "the huge CPU burden that restricts GTA 6 to 30fps on even PS5 Pro" — so how would the comparatively little guy Switch 2 ever stand a chance?

Now, we're never willing to rule anything out completely. Switch 1 got its fair share of 'impossible ports' in its lifetime, and Switch 2 has already landed a handful of beefy boys that we previously never could have imagined making the jump intact. However, this one might be a step too far. Please do prove us wrong, Rockstar!

It's a shame, because by all accounts, the game is fantastic. Our sister sites Push Square and Pure Xbox both gave it 10/10 reviews, praising it as one of the greatest open worlds of all time. It shot right to the top of Metacritic's ratings, too, tying with Ocarina of Time as the best of the best.

Last of the old school buyers, Publicis Media vet John Muszynski prepares to retire

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

He’s equally comfortable at the negotiating table or on skates in an ice rink.

He doesn’t do it himself but he knows principal media is part of the business.

He’s said to have once humbled the CEO of a major cable network group by holding back $200 million of his clients’ media spend when that CEO tried to bully him into big price increases.

He’s the longest-serving head of investment in media. And now, John Muszynski, legendary media investment executive with Publicis Media, plans to retire.

After 45 years at one holding company and negotiating nearly as many upfronts over that span, the chairman of Publicis Media Exchange (PMX) U.S., is stepping down from a full-time role this year, although he plans to continue to consult with the holdco.

Chicago-based Muszynski, who oversees decision-making on what Publicis estimates to be 40% of the U.S. video marketplace inventory, is arguably the last of the old-school heads of investment that have seen the upfronts morph into the byzantine bazaar it is today.

“For nearly five decades, I’ve had a front row seat to the industry’s transformation,” Muszynski told Digiday. “It’s gone from three networks to always-on content spanning the entire digital ecosystem. It’s been fascinating to be a part of it all.”

The avid ice hockey fan, hunter and fisherman (when he’s not negotiating) got his start in 1981 as a planner for Leo Burnett’s burgeoning media unit, handling longtime Publicis clients including Kimberly-Clark, McDonald’s and Kellogg’s. That unit eventually became standalone media agency Starcom, for which he rose to CEO of Starcom USA from 2005-2009. He then helped to build Spark Foundry from a 65-person boutique into a 3,000-person media agency.

In 2016, Publicis formed Publicis Media Exchange, which harnessed the collective buying clout of what was then Starcom, Mediavest, Spark, Zenith, Optimedia and Blue 449 media agencies. He was the lead negotiator when that formed, and became chairman of the group in 2022.

The execs who sat across from him at the negotiating table said they always felt they got a fair shake — in part because Muszynski always came prepared, was knowledgable and true to his word.

“John has been a tremendous influence on generations of marketers, agency team members and publishers,” said Mark Marshall, chairman of global advertising and partnerships at NBCUniversal. “He has always put his clients’ objectives at the forefront and pushed all of us to deliver for those clients. He is the epitome of ‘a handshake is his word’.”

Rita Ferro, president, global advertising, disney, was even more personal in her assessment of working with Muszynski. “John is a legend in this business and from the moment we met, he became a trusted partner and more importantly a dear friend,” she said.

Sean Moran, COO at Evergreen Trading agency, who negotiated with Muszynski during his 24 years as an ad sales exec with Viacom until 2020, thinks of Muszynski as a contemporary of the old-school buyers of the TV business, from Grey Advertising’s Jon Mandel to UM’s Donna Wolfe, and from Omnicom’s John Swift to GroupM’s Rino Scanzoni.

“When I was running MTV and we had a big upfront event, and had to get people there, if I could get John Muszynski to fly in and come to my event, that was a huge win,” said Moran. ”He represented so much gravitas. Obviously he controlled a lot of budgets, but it was also because he was so smart and yet had such empathy for good people. You couldn’t bullshit him. He was a good Midwestern guy who could talk about a great deal over over a steak, but he did his homework and knew his numbers.”

Moran said Muszynski often set the marketplace as “the main controller” of the upfronts. “One of his negotiating tactics was, ‘Yeah, I haven’t seen everything, but boy, it looks down’,” said Moran. “If he respected your inventory, you could do a deal with him fairly quickly and cut through the chaff. He was known to pull that with one or two people that were at the front of the line, and protect his clients. He would therefore set the marketplace for himself. It was very smart.”

Although Muszynski will retire from his full-time position at PMX, he will stay on to consult through 2027, working with Joel Lunenfeld who leads PMX globally and Mark Griffin, who oversees all investment in the U.S. as chief investment officer.

Mediaplus and Publicis clients, including Banco Santander, have paused TikTok spending in wake of investigations into abuse images circulating on the video platform.

Madison and Wall estimates creator marketing growing at double the pace of the rest of advertising, driven on by creator-led production investments.

A new crop of companies promise to coordinate advertising budgets across platforms, but can they control buying decisions, or merely add another interface to the existing system?

GTA 6 has barely been out for a day, but players have already speedrun to reach Lucia and Jason's maximum weight limits. Turns out, neither of their biggest weights is that big at all, so I have a sneaking suspicion the inevitable PC mods will likely turn them into absolute units.

Mere hours after the game's midnight launch, Twitter user Sir Joseph Windsor posted their "Overweight Strong" Jason (below), a version of the character that seemed to have equal parts body fat and muscle. A solid diet of high-strength weight workouts and fast food, no doubt.

The GTA 6 fat speedrunner then posted a brief guide for anyone looking to also put meat onto Jason or Lucia's bones. First, you need to eat at least four burgers or tacos from the game's various fast food chains. Then go to sleep. Then stuff your face with more fast food. Damaging your vehicle will soon prompt you to repair it, which will take you to a loading screen where, hopefully, your character's calories have sunk in. And, voila, apparently. Important note: you'll want to avoid doing workouts as either character for this to work.

Tokenized Stocks Shrug Off the Crypto Selloff

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Sign up to get 2 days early access to each edition of Tokenization Briefing

Bitcoin fell as low as $62k late Saturday, its sixth straight weekly decline. Meanwhile, tokenized stocks crossed $4 billion in onchain value for the first time on Nov 11. Tokenized stocks are now roughly five times their value on January 1.

Tokenized stocks track equity prices, not crypto, so a falling BTC doesn’t directly pull their prices down. What stands out, however, is that crypto users appear to keep buying them through the selloff. Demand for 24/7 trading and onchain access to US equities appears to have found strong product-market fit as they are holding up even as the rest of the crypto market weakens.

In September, rising share prices drove most of the growth in value of tokenized stocks. Since mid-October, new minting has driven the growth in value even as share prices dropped. Over the eleven weeks to Nov 13, minting added $1.12 billion while price moves took away $339 million. Binance’s bStocks accounted for about four fifths of that minting, led by NVIDIA, SpaceX and Circle.

Market value has stalled over the past two weeks. We expect minting to pick back up again with the launch of OKXICE on Nov 23 opening large-cap tokenized stocks to US retail.

Securitize reported $11.5 million in tokenization revenue in Q3, its highest on record and 28% more than Q3 2025. Securitize's average tokenized assets reached a record $5 billion as of Q3 as BlackRock, Ark Invest and Neuberger brought new products onchain.

Tokenization revenue is more variable because it rises and falls with issuance, while asset servicing has settled around $7 million a quarter. Securitize's acquisition of River One could help smooth out those revenue swings and diversify its top line. Lending against tokenized assets adds recurring income on top of issuance. We expect credit to show up as Securitize's third revenue line within a year of the deal closing.

Most of the roughly $4 billion of tokenized stock sits in wrappers that US investors can't buy. Only about 10% is open to American investors, and almost all of that is crypto companies including Securitize, Figure, Exodus and Forward Industries. NVIDIA, SpaceX and the other large names are effectively offshore only.

That could change on Nov 23, when OKXICE opens to US customers who have onboarded through its CeDeFi Wallet. It’s the first venue built for American retail at scale, so the onshore share should grow post-launch. We expect issuer-backed listings to drive it, with 67 companies having opted out.

MegaRobo raises nearly $200M to let AI run real lab experiments

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Oman's sovereign wealth fund joins a round for robot labs that test what AI designs.

Chinese lab automation company MegaRobo Technologies has raised nearly $200M in a round joined by the Oman Investment Authority and CMB International. It builds systems that let AI design experiments, run them in robotic labs and learn from the results, and plans to expand across North America, Europe, the Middle East and Asia.

China’s MegaRobo Technologies has raised nearly $200M to build labs where AI can run its own experiments, the company announced on Thursday. The Oman Investment Authority, the country’s sovereign wealth fund, joined the round, along with new investor CMB International.

Existing backers Asia Investment Capital, Granite Asia and Sinovation Ventures also put in more money. MegaRobo did not disclose its valuation.

AI can read papers and predict molecules, but science still needs real tests. MegaRobo links AI models to robots and lab tools. A model can plan a test, run it and use the results to pick the next one.

“That shift changes AI from a system that interprets the world into one that can help create new knowledge, materials, processes and products,” said founder and CEO Daniel Huang.

MegaRobo says it has more than 500 customers, mostly drug makers, biotech firms and research labs. Its Megalaxy Lab lets scientists and AI agents send tests to robot labs, and has served more than 200 of them.

It has also launched Poreva Open, a protein design contest with Tencent Cloud and DNA maker Twist Bioscience. Megalaxy Lab will test more than 10,000 AI-designed proteins in a real lab, to set a shared benchmark built on actual results. Anthropic, for one, had outside labs check Claude’s protein designs in August.

Money is pouring into AI drug design. Alphabet’s Isomorphic Labs raised $5B on Tuesday, and Chai Discovery raised $400M in July. In Berlin, Dunia Innovations is building a €280M robotic lab to test what AI designs.

MegaRobo says it has raised about $600M in total, including a $300M Series C backed by Goldman Sachs in 2022. It plans to expand across North America, Europe, the Middle East and Asia, and to use the same technology beyond science, much like Jeff Bezos’ physical AI lab.

AI features could be turned off with a simple toggle, Canva proves

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AI has taken over everything in the past few years, with AI features bombarding virtually every app or service available. If you’ve ever wanted to just turn off AI features, Canva proves that it could be a simple toggle, if companies actually wanted to give you a choice.

Canva today announced “AI Controls,” a new toggle that completely turns off generative AI features within the editing app.

Generative AI can be quite helpful in working on posters, editing images, and more, but it’s not everyone’s preference. Canva puts this well:

For some people, AI is the most exciting creative tool in a generation, while others prefer bringing their ideas to life without it. We believe the future of creativity should give everyone the freedom to choose their own path, whether that’s creating with AI, without it, or somewhere in between.

To give its users the choice, Canva’s Permissions menu now has a simple toggle for “Enable Generative AI features.” Toggling it off disables features such as Magic Write and Magic Edit. There’s also an option to limit how much AI-generated imagery appears in the Canva library.

AI has its place, but everyone should have a choice like this, especially when it comes to apps and services used for artistry in the same vein as Canva. Yet, the tech industry continues to go in the opposite direction, shoving AI into every corner whether it’s actually useful or not. Canva proves, though, that it doesn’t have to be that way.

Anthropic stock jumps 34% at the open of its record-breaking Nasdaq debut

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The AI company's stock opened at $111.50 against an $83 IPO price before dipping below $100 at one point

Anthropic opened trading on the Nasdaq $NDAQ at $111.50 a share on Thursday, 34% above its $83 IPO price, in the debut of its stock under the ticker ANT. The offering is the largest IPO in history, according to CNN.

The stock hit a high of $115.10 after the open before falling below $100 at one point. At another point it traded at $101.24, up 22% from the IPO price.

Anthropic priced the offering on Wednesday at 980 million shares of Class A common stock at $83 each, a size of $81.34 billion, according to a filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission. Anthropic offered 956,351,354 of the shares, and selling stockholders offered 23,648,646. Underwriters hold an option to buy up to 147 million additional shares. Settlement is scheduled for Nov. 20.

The company also arranged a concurrent private placement of $26.0 billion of Class N common stock at the same $83 price, the filing said. Goldman Sachs $GS and Morgan Stanley $MS are the lead bookrunning managers.

The Nasdaq Composite had gained as much as 2.1% during the morning before turning lower as Anthropic began trading. The S&P 500 slid 1.5% and the Nasdaq shed 1.4%, while the Dow surrendered 1,029 points, a decline of over 2%. Shares of Nvidia $NVDA and SpaceX fell 1.9% and 5.3%, respectively.

CEO Dario Amodei and President Daniela Amodei rang the opening bell at the Nasdaq. "If we can show it's possible to make the right choices on safety and still build an incredibly successful company, others in our industry will be inspired to do the same," Dario Amodei said in remarks before the bell, according to CNN.

The company set its IPO price at $83 a share on Wednesday, which values it at about $1.5 trillion on a fully diluted basis. The offering exceeds the $75 billion SpaceX raised at the start of its June IPO. SpaceX's total reached $85.7 billion once its underwriters took up their option to sell additional shares.

The $26 billion private placement was with seven tech companies. After it, Amazon $AMZN holds close to 15% of Anthropic's shares outstanding, and Google $GOOGL holds roughly 14%.

According to its prospectus, Anthropic brought in $33.24 billion in revenue over the nine months ended Sept. 30, a 1,200% increase from the same period a year earlier.

Anthropic's registration statement said founders, directors, officers, certain employees and certain stockholders have signed lock-up agreements that restrict transfers of company stock in connection with the offering.

A vanished bird from Haiti may help solve an ancient Pacific mystery

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

DNA reveals how snipes and woodcocks reached distant islands, stopped migrating, changed physically, and became vulnerable to extinction.

The sandpiper family includes some of the most extreme long-distance migrants among birds.

Two branches of that family, the snipes and the woodcocks, have behaved quite differently. These birds reached islands again and again, and migration was then abandoned.

Nearly every living species was examined in a new DNA study.

The results suggest that island settlement was accompanied by changes in body size and flying ability. Island species later proved vulnerable when people arrived with rats and cats.

The work was led by Min Zhao, then a postdoctoral researcher at the Florida Museum of Natural History. Colleagues at Virginia Tech and the Field Museum in Chicago also took part.

According to Zhao, the whole project grew out of one extinct bird from Hispaniola. That Caribbean island is shared by Haiti and the Dominican Republic.

The dates reported in the study were estimated from family trees built with DNA. The uncertainty attached to them can run to millions of years.

Florida Museum researchers dug the bird’s bones out of a cave in Haiti in 1984.

In 2015, those bones were described by two scientists as a new species of woodcock. Woodcocks are long-billed birds that probe soft soil for food.

One of the two scientists was David Steadman, who is now an emeritus curator at the museum. He is also listed as a co-author of the new study.

Zhao’s team extracted DNA from two of the fossil specimens. The researchers then read the mitochondrial genome, a small set of genes passed from mother to offspring.

Both specimens were placed with the snipes, which form the sister group of the woodcocks. Their closest living relative turned out to be the giant snipe of lowland South America.

The analysis suggested a South American origin for the Haitian bird’s ancestors. The two lineages are thought to have split between 5 and 12 million years ago.

Why, then, did the bones resemble those of a woodcock? The authors think the bird’s build may have been altered by island living, which could explain the mix-up.

The outer wing bone of the Haitian bird was unusually short when compared with its upper wing bone. The authors wrote that it could fly but probably not well.

The team sequenced 45 samples in total. Two were the Haitian fossils and 19 were toe pads taken from old museum specimens. The remaining 24 were fresh tissue samples.

All but one of the 29 living species of snipes and woodcocks were covered by these samples. Specimens were supplied by ten museums and research collections.

A family tree was built from the data, and both groups were traced back to Eurasia.

The ancestors of today’s American birds crossed from the Eastern Hemisphere to the Western Hemisphere four separate times.

One of those crossings took place roughly 20 million years ago. It was then that the ancestor of the American woodcock reached North America.

The researchers could not tell whether the bird arrived across the Atlantic or by way of Alaska.

The Austral snipes are found on the outlying islands of New Zealand. Scientists have long placed these small brown birds in a group of their own.

Some snipes from Eurasia spend each winter in Australia and on nearby islands. The DNA showed that those visitors are not close relatives of the Austral snipes.

Instead, the Austral snipes share a branch with several large South American snipes and the extinct Haitian bird.

Two DNA datasets placed the split between the Austral snipes and their relatives at about 14 million and nearly 18 million years ago, respectively.

The southern continents had broken apart long before either estimate. The snipes’ ancestor most likely crossed the Pacific.

How the crossing happened remains an open question. The authors suggested that birds straying far outside their normal range may have played a role. Such straying was perhaps caused by extreme weather.

The authors also raised a route through Antarctica. Patches of treeless tundra are thought to have lasted there until at least 12.8 million years ago.

Several living snipe species still breed on tundra in the Arctic.

The analysis indicated that the ancestors of both snipes and woodcocks were migrants. Their island descendants are not.

Ten of the 29 living species are found only on islands, and none of those ten migrates.

The habit was lost several times. Woodcocks gave it up when they settled the Malay Archipelago in Southeast Asia. Snipes did the same when they settled South America and Madagascar.

Body size changed as well, although not in a single direction.

The Amami woodcock of southern Japan is about 45% heavier than the Eurasian woodcock, its closest mainland relative. Madagascar’s snipe is about 91% heavier than the common snipe of Eurasia.

The opposite change is seen in the Austral snipes. Their average weight is 3.4 ounces (96 grams), which is about the weight of a deck of cards.

South American snipes on the same branch average 8.6 ounces (243.5 grams). That is more than twice as much.

The smaller size may be explained by scarce food, the authors suggested. Competition from kiwis and other birds that probe the soil for worms was also considered possible.

Ten species of snipes and woodcocks are known to have died out in the recent geologic past. Nine of them lived on islands.

The Austral snipes suffered the heaviest losses. Three of nine known species survive. All three are ranked as Vulnerable or Near Threatened by the IUCN Red List.

The South Island snipe died out in 1964, and introduced black rats are regarded as the likely cause.

No resident snipe or woodcock is left in the Caribbean. At least three species have been lost there.

The youngest dated bones of the Haitian bird are about 600 years old. Rats and feral cats brought by people are suspected of causing its extinction.

Snipes nest on the ground, where they can be reached by those predators. Island species had given up migration, and in some cases strong flight as well.

The authors wrote that these changes made such species especially vulnerable to human impacts.

Overhunting was named as one of those impacts.

Jessica Oswald, a co-author, also worked on the study at the Florida Museum.

“People will eat anything,” said Oswald. She said a crew of hungry explorers or whalers could wipe out an island’s birds in a single weekend.

Island woodcocks in Japan and Southeast Asia appear to have fared better. They live in mountain forests, which may be harder for people to reach.

New Zealand may have been reached first by the Austral snipes.

From there they could have spread to Norfolk Island, New Caledonia and Fiji. The opposite path cannot be ruled out, because no genetic data exist for those islands’ extinct snipes.

More snipe species may once have lived in the Pacific and are now extinct. In that case, today’s birds would be the last survivors of a larger group. Direct fossil evidence of that link across the Pacific is still missing.

The Haitian bird’s place on the tree rests on mitochondrial genes from two specimens. A possible close relative from Puerto Rico is also extinct and has not been tested genetically.

The authors suggest that DNA from fossils yet to be found on Pacific islands could help trace how snipes spread across the region.

The full study was published in the journal Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences.

I just found a curious artifact of the Old West in GTA 6, and the best part is I stumbled across it completely by accident.

That's right, partner: Arthur Morgan's cowboy hat is in Grand Theft Auto 6, and you won't find it in some trendy Western fashion shop or in a McMansion as part of a wealthy crypto bro's collection.

If you want to wear it yourself, I'll tell you below where you can find it.

I was out in Port Gellhorn looking for a particular kind of car for reasons completely unrelated to clothing accessories. While I was driving, I spotted a sign on the side of the road with a balloon tied to it, reading "Yard Sale" with an arrow pointing down the road.

I followed the sign which led to more yard sale signs and, yup, sure enough, the signs pointed me to an actual yard sale. And among the items for sale was a very familiar-looking hat.

It's called a "Genuine Cowboy Hat" and, this being a yard sale, it only cost $5. But I knew the second I saw it that this was no ordinary cowboy hat. Here, have a look at it from the back.

Yep. No mistaking that. I even got a little pang of sadness thinking about good ol' Arthur Morgan. I miss him. Maybe when I'm done with GTA 6, I'll strap on my spurs and take another ride through Red Dead Redemption 2.

Directions: Head to Port Gellhorn. It's in Kelly County on the western side of the state along the coast. In the southern tip of Port Gellhorn, there's a small neighborhood of about five houses just northwest of the Dirt Merchants mod shop.

Look for the driveway filled with yard sale clutter: furniture and electronics and such. There's a bunch of balloons, a cut-out of a nearly naked cowboy, and a big American flag flying. You'll find the hat toward the back sitting on top of a guitar case on a couch. There's also a shirt, a cap, and some shoes for sale that definitely didn't belong to Arthur.

Hyundai Reveals Full Specs on the Radically Redesigned Elantra

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The 2027 Elantra compact sedan and the 2028 Elantra hybrid bring new styling, additional features, and improved mpg.

The most notable thing about Hyundai’s new Elantra is its radical redesign, but there are enough updates under the skin to make this new compact sedan compelling for other reasons. The 2027 Elantra will arrive first with a mostly carryover engine, but the 2028 Elantra Hybrid comes with upgrades to its gas-electric powertrain that improve its power and mpg figures.

We already saw the Elantra’s new sci-fi styling a few months ago when it was revealed globally, and the newly revealed U.S. version looks nearly the same. Angular lighting elements, sharply creased body lines, and futuristic details make this look far more distinctive than anything else in the mainstream compact segment. It’s also larger than before, with the wheelbase stretching by 1.2 inches, which provides extra legroom in the rear seat.

See all results for new 2027 Hyundai Elantra for sale near 10011

The standard model, arriving for the 2027 model year, has a carryover powertrain consisting of a 147-hp 2.0-liter inline-four and a continuously variable automatic transmission. The previous model offered a more powerful turbocharged engine in the N Line variant, but Hyundai has yet to announce any sort of performance version of the new Elantra—though we assume the full-bore Elantra N will eventually join the new generation.

The 2028 Elantra Hybrid has more power than before; its combination of a 1.6-liter inline-four and electric motors now makes 155 horsepower, an uptick of 16 horses compared with before. Hyundai estimates it will achieve up to 53 mpg combined, which is 1 mpg less than the outgoing model’s highest combined rating. It’s also notable that the hybrid model has a multilink rear suspension setup, compared with the nonhybrid’s twist-beam rear suspension.

We like the look of the new interior, at least in the loaded Limited model we sat in, which incorporates nicer materials than you might expect in this segment. The dashboard layout is fresh and modern, with a standard 12.9-inch touchscreen protruding from the center of the dash. There’s also an optional slim digital gauge cluster that’s located near the base of the windshield. Hyundai’s latest Pleos Connect infotainment is in play here too, and it’s said to recognize more natural voice commands. Optional features include dual wireless charging pads and a Bang & Olufsen stereo.

The ‘27 Elantra lineup starts with the base SE trim level and also includes SEL Sport and Limited trims. The hybrid lineup is similar, but the base model is called SE Blue. The outgoing Elantra is one of the most affordable cars on sale, and we hope the new one’s starting price won’t rise much above $25,000. The ‘28 Elantra hybrid, arriving later on, will cost a few thousand dollars more than that.

See all results for new 2027 Hyundai Elantra for sale near 10011

The 2027 Hyundai Elantra Is Bigger and Much, Much Bolder

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A sharp-edged “Art of Steel” design gives the Korean compact unmistakable panache.

The Hyundai Elantra continues to defy the apparent incipient death of the sedan, and the Korean automaker just gave it a major upgrade for 2027. Between a new “Art of Steel” design, a larger size, and upgraded infotainment technology, the 2027 Hyundai Elantra is more prepared for the future than ever before.

Revealed Thursday at the 2026 Los Angeles Auto Show, the 2027 Elantra's most obvious feature is its exterior design. Created under Hyundai's new Art of Steel design philosophy, which was first revealed on the Boulder concept, the Elantra is now a boxy beast with wide fenders, sharp headlights, and a square rear end. This new sedan was designed with a very clear three-box configuration and sport sedan dynamism in mind, according to Hyundai’s head of design.

“There is an inherent beauty in a sedan because its proportions are honest, intuitive and functional,” said SangYup Lee, executive vice president and head of the Hyundai and Genesis Global Design Center. “With the all-new Elantra, we returned to that essence, then pushed it forward. Wide, muscular fenders, a long hood, and refined three-box proportions give Elantra a confident, athletic presence that challenges the conventions of the compact sedan.”

In addition to the new exterior design, changes under the skin mean the latest Elantra is now 3.3 inches longer, 1.1 inches wider, and features a 1.1-inch longer wheelbase than its predecessor; all told, Hyundai says the 2027 Elantra has over one inch more rear headroom and legroom. So-called H-Edge lighting, futuristic grille styling, slim- neck mirrors, and lift-up flush door handles make up the rest of the visual changes.

But Hyundai didn’t stop its design renovations with the exterior. Inside the Elantra, the carmaker added a new Curve of Upholstery design and an asymmetrical front layout, which is meant to “gently embrace” the driver. A central 12.9-inch Pleos Connect infotainment system is standard on all Elantra models, while an available slim information display in the driver’s natural line of sight provides critical driving information. Other interior features include 15-kW dual wireless phone charging, Audio by Bang & Olufsen, Hyundai Digital Key 2, and 100-watt ultra-fast charging USB ports. Upholstery will be available in four black color combinations, as well as Burgundy/Rose Ivory and Chalk Beige.

Mechanically, the Elantra is a bit less exciting. The new model will offer two powertrain options: a 1.6-liter hybrid four-cylinder powerplant, and a naturally-aspirated 2.0-liter four-cylinder engine. The hybrid model will deliver 154 horsepower through a six-speed dual-clutch transmission, while the purely internal combustion model offers 146 horsepower routed through a continuously variable transmission. Hybridized Elantra models also feature a predictive drive system that intelligently anticipates driving patterns and road conditions to maximize efficiency, as well as a new “Stay Mode” that lets the climate control, stereo, charging ports and other non-motion-related functions operate for up to 30 minutes with the engine off.

The pure ICE version will arrive as a 2027 model year Elantra, while the new Hyundai Elantra Hybrid will debut as a 2028 model year vehicle. Specific on-sale timing, pricing, fuel economy ratings and other details will be revealed closer to the vehicle’s American launch.

Jimmy Kimmel Had More Trump Jokes, But I Didn’t See The CMAs Jokes Coming

If you’ve been watching Jimmy Kimmel Live! – or if not live on social media – you should know commentary on President Trump and the White House in general has become an almost nightly occurrence. There’s a lot of history behind the contention, from Kimmel’s pointed jokes, to Trump and the White House’s bold insults, and FCC involvement in between. So, a Trump segment on late night? Not unexpected. Random shots thrown at the CMAs? A bit more surprising.

Last night Kimmel did spend a fair chunk of his monologue focusing on President Trump and Trump's recent visit to China. Though he made some comments about Communism, the weirdest thing he noticed about the visit was that the President seems to be wearing makeup on his hands these days to give him a little more youthful vigor.

This hand has been paved over more times than the Kennedy Center. Either that is makeup or he's eating peanut butter straight out of the jar again.

The Trump bit went on for a while in more traditional Jimmy Kimmel Live! style, and we'll have to wait and see if the President responds. Still, I was more amused this week by Kimmel trying to get in on the CMAs commentary. Let's get into the bobsledding joke, shall we?

OK, maybe I shouldn’t have been as surprised as I was to see Kimmel commenting on country music given the CMAs aired on ABC with a big, splashy Taylor Swift appearance (that led to some Swiftie theories). That's his own network, and it's nice to see him playing ball, even if some rumors have called his relationship with the network "unmanageable."

Sorry to everyone named Luke in Nashville, because Kimmel had all the jokes about some of last night’s major winners, including a sly reference to Luke Combs being in a long list of male country singers named Luke, also including The Voice's own Luke Bryan. (That whole convo gets weird too because while not named Luke, Zach Bryan is also a famous country singer. None of these people are related.)

The CMAs are the single biggest night of the year for guys named Luke. Number 1! Luke Combs scored three nominations, three more than his brother Sean Diddy, they had a tribute to Dolly Parton, of course. Brooks and Dunn got the Lifetime Achievement Award. It’s the highest honor in country music, named after the highest singer.

That last bit is a Willie Nelson joke, which is quite funny, but only if you already know the Lifetime Achievement Award is named for him. I did not see it coming though when Kimmel brought up bobsledding in regards to “Choosin' Texas.”

The big story this year is Ella Langley. She made history, her song “Choosin' Texas” spent 28 weeks at Number 1 on the Billboard charts, more than any song ever, and it’s still going. It’s still the No. 1 song. It’s been No. 1 since the Winter Olympics, which was mid February. This song has been No. 1 since the last time we thought about bobsledding!

Yeah, that’s a joke, but it’s also just the kind of absurd fact that’s worth remarking on. Ella Langley's crushing it, and this milestone is cool, but now I'll never think of it without also thinking of Olympics bobsledding. Sure, these are some fairly light jokes compared to his Trump stuff, but please don't say Mr. Kimmel never did anything for pop culture.

2027 Hyundai Elantra Feels Too Nice To Be Treated Like An Economy Car

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

For the most part, economy cars are treated as fairly disposable pieces of equipment meant mostly for getting folks from Point A to Point B. They're not exactly treated with much care during their lives, and when they're used up, they're scuttled off to the nearest junkyard. More likely than not, that same fate will befall the 2027 Hyundai Elantra and 2028 Elantra Hybrid, and I don't think that's fair. Top to bottom and front to back, this little sedan is just far too nice to deserve a fate like that.

As with the redesigned Tucson, Hyundai put its entire back into its eighth-generation compact sedan, giving it a distinct and decidedly upmarket look, a premium-feeling interior, a cutting-edge brain with stellar interior tech, and a hybrid powertrain that'll get over 50 mpg, all while maintaining the idea that "affordability is standard," according to Olabisi Boyle, Hyundai's Senior VP of product planning and mobility strategy. While I haven't driven it yet, the slightly larger new Elantra seems mighty impressive when it comes to the design, feel and specs.

Full Disclosure: Hyundai paid for my food and transportation so I could check out the new Elantra and Tucson at a warehouse in Jersey City, New Jersey.

The first thing about the new Elantra that will jump out at you about is undoubtedly how it looks. It's the latest example of Hyundai's "Art of Steel" design language that we've previously seen on the China-only Ioniq V, Boulder concept and recently revealed Tucson. Hyundai also told me that the Elantra's design pulls from the N Vision 74 concept, and even though this is a totally different kind of car, you can certainly see those cues, especially in the wide fenders of the rear three-quarter section. The high-performance N version of the Elantra has already been spied, too, and it's going to look sick as hell.

The Elantra is slightly bigger than it used to be; 3.4 inches longer and 1.2 inches wider, to be exact, with 1.2 inches added to the wheelbase. Hyundai says the majority of that extra space goes to the second row, with gains in legroom (1.1 inches), headroom (0.7 inches) and shoulder room for rear-seat occupants, and now folks who accidentally had a kid will be able to better fit a rear-facing car seat.

Despite the extra size and funky angles, the 2027 Elantra still cuts through the air with ease, achieving a 0.26 coefficient of drag. Apparently, H-shaped daytime running lights and taillights are very aerodynamic. Who knew? The wedgey front end, also reminiscent of the N Vision 74, doesn't hurt either, and neither do the flush-mounted door handles.

It all helps explain why the Elantra is a real fuel economy winner whether you're looking at the entry-level gas motor or the optional hybrid. Fitted with 16-inch wheels, the 147-horsepower 2.0-liter inline-4, with the help of a CVT, will return up to 35 mpg combined, according to Hyundai. The 155-hp hybrid's 1.6-liter four-cylinder is mated to a six-speed dual-clutch transmission and 1.49-kWh battery, and Hyundai says it's going to achieve up to 53 mpg combined, which puts it firmly in "really, really good" territory. There's no word on other performance metrics right now, but I wouldn't expect anything too exciting, if I'm honest. The hybrid also comes with a Stay mode that lets you run accessories off the hybrid battery rather than the ICE motor when stationary for longer periods of time.

While the exterior is full of hard edges and sudden cuts, the interior is a far softer, more inviting place, one that wouldn't look out of position on a car that costs far more than the Elantra's currently unannounced price would suggest. Hyundai calls the furniture-inspired design ethos "Curve of Upholstery," and it's supposed to be all about comfort, simplicity, and minimizing driver distractions. It not only looks pretty thanks to interesting styling and materials choices, but it also feels nice to the touch with solid build quality.

Hyundai says Elantra is a "software-defined vehicle," which is pretty uncommon for an internal-combustion-powered car. Mostly Hyundai's spokespeople talk about its Pleos Connect operating system and its capabilities, like next-gen navigation (and AI assistance crap), and it explains why the interior's focal point is the 12.9-inch infotainment touchscreen in the center of the dash. The left section of the screen serves as a driver display showing speed and other pieces of vehicle information, while the rest of it is a regular infotainment screen with configurable widgets for media, maps, and everything else you can imagine — including things you can't imagine, since Pleos allows owners to download apps created by others.

Beneath the center screen are dual wireless 15-watt Qi 2.0 chargers and 100-watt ultra-fast USB-C charging ports for your many important devices, and you can run audio from said devices into an optional 12-speaker Bang & Olufsen audio system. Not too shabby.

Then, behind the steering wheel is a slim 9.9-inch display that is meant to keep the driver's field of vision uncluttered and only display critical information like speed, as well as radio, map directions or a few other things via widgets. We first saw both of these screens in the Tucson, and they make the Elantra feel far more tech-forward than a usual compact sedan would. Adding to that tech-forwardness is Hyundai's SmartSense suite of driver-assistance systems, which includes the classics like lane-keeping assist, lane centering, adaptive cruise control, forward collision avoidance and blind-spot monitoring.

At launch, the gas-only Elantra will come in SE, SEL Sport, and Limited flavors. As you may imagine, features and materials get nicer as you move up the range, but the 12.9-inch screen, adaptive cruise control, push-button start and a few other niceties are all standard from the get-go. Coming a bit later (no word on how much later) will be the 2028 Elantra Hybrid, which will come in SE Blue, SEL Sport, and Limited trims. It's the same deal as the gas car, but there's even more as standard, including dual-zone climate control and Stay mode.

I know that right now the Elantra is shiny and new, with its covers just pulled off at the 2026 LA Auto Show, and soon it'll face the sorts of abuse that all budget-friendly cars do. Before that happens, enjoy the Elantra and what it has to offer the world, because it's not very often an automaker swings for the fences on a cheap vehicle like this. Yes, vehicles like the Corolla, Civic and Mazda3 are very solid, but they're never exactly inspiring — certainly not from a design perspective, at least.

The eighth-generation Elantra elevates the segment, and it might force other automakers to rethink their budget vehicles as well. It certainly looks polarizing from the outside, and it won't be for everyone, but it's also distinctive, and that's something that is very hard to come by on a budget. If you're the type of person who appreciates that, plus a comfortable interior and tech that punches well above its weight class, then it's best you start getting ready now, because the Elantra is set to hit U.S. dealers in early 2027.

Mozilla: Staff Software Engineer, OS Integration

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mozilla Corporation is the non-profit-backed technology company that has shaped the internet for the better over the last 25 years. We make pioneering brands like Firefox, the privacy-minded web browser. Now, with more than 225 million people around the world using our products each month, we’re shaping the next 25 years of technology and helping to reclaim an internet built for people, not companies. Our work focuses on diverse areas including AI, social media, security and more. And we’re doing this while never losing our focus on our core mission – to make the internet better for people.

The Mozilla Corporation is wholly owned by the non-profit 501(c) Mozilla Foundation. This means we aren’t beholden to any shareholders — only to our mission. Along with thousands of volunteer contributors and collaborators all over the world, Mozillians design, build and distribute open-source software that enables people to enjoy the internet on their terms.

The OS Integration team ensures Firefox works well with the host operating system and environment. We’re a distributed team with expertise in Windows, macOS, and Linux-based operating systems. Our responsibilities include developing fixes and improvements for Firefox related to Windows, macOS, and Linux environments. This might include integration with a new OS feature or fixing an OS-specific user interface bug. Other responsibilities include native crash reporting, OS abstraction layers, sandboxing, hardening, installer, updater, device APIs, and enterprise DLP support.

As a member of the OS Integration team, you’ll develop fixes, improvements, and new features for Firefox. The role will lean on your Windows expertise, but projects may have a cross-platform component. This role will include development of features for Firefox in enterprise environments especially implementation of Data Loss Prevention (DLP) features. As a Staff Software Engineer, you’re a clear communicator and expert in your domain, ready to work on multi-month projects. You’ll contribute to the team through code review, mentoring, and discussion.

Mozilla exists to build the Internet as a public resource accessible to all because we believe that open and free is better than closed and controlled. When you work at Mozilla, you give yourself a chance to make a difference in the lives of Web users everywhere. And you give us a chance to make a difference in your life every single day. Join us to work on the Web as the platform and help create more opportunity and innovation for everyone online.

Commitment to diversity, equity, inclusion, and belonging

Mozilla understands that valuing diverse creative practices and forms of knowledge are crucial to and enrich the company’s core mission.  We encourage applications from everyone, including members of all equity-seeking communities, such as (but certainly not limited to) women, racialized and Indigenous persons, persons with disabilities, persons of all sexual orientations, gender identities, and expressions.

We will ensure that qualified individuals with disabilities are provided reasonable accommodations to participate in the job application or interview process, to perform essential job functions, and to receive other benefits and privileges of employment, as appropriate. Please contact us at [email protected] to request accommodation.

We are an equal opportunity employer. We do not discriminate on the basis of race (including hairstyle and texture), religion (including religious grooming and dress practices), gender, gender identity, gender expression, color, national origin, pregnancy, ancestry, domestic partner status, disability, sexual orientation, age, genetic predisposition, medical condition, marital status, citizenship status, military or veteran status, or any other basis covered by applicable laws.  Mozilla will not tolerate discrimination or harassment based on any of these characteristics or any other unlawful behavior, conduct, or purpose.

No forms. Just smart, optimized applications.

Remember every detail and next step, without a meeting bot.

🇬🇧 United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland
